Enumerating k k -SAT functions Thanks: Mani was supported by the NSF Graduate Research Fellowship Program and a Hertz Graduate Fellowship. Thanks: Zhao was supported by NSF award DMS-1764176, NSF CAREER award DMS-2044606, a Sloan Research Fellowship, and the MIT Solomon Buchsbaum Fund.
Dingding Dong, Nitya Mani, Yufei Zhao

Abstract
. How many k k -SAT functions on n n boolean variables are there? What does a typical such function look like? Bollobás, Brightwell, and Leader conjectured that, for each fixed k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , the number of k k -SAT functions on n n variables is ( 1 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ 2 ( n k ) + n (1+o(1))2^{\binom{n}{k}+n} , or equivalently: a 1 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) 1-o(1) fraction of all k k -SAT functions are unate, i.e., monotone after negating some variables. They proved a weaker version of the conjecture for k = 2 k=2 . The conjecture was confirmed for k = 2 k=2 by Allen and k = 3 k=3 by Ilinca and Kahn. We show that the problem of enumerating k k -SAT functions is equivalent to a Turán density problem for partially directed hypergraphs. Our proof uses the hypergraph container method. Furthermore, we confirm the Bollobás–Brightwell–Leader conjecture for k = 4 k=4 by solving the corresponding Turán density problem. Our solution applies a recent result of Füredi and Maleki on the minimum triangular edge density in a graph of given edge density. In an appendix (by Nitya Mani and Edward Yu), we further confirm the k = 5 k=5 case of the conjecture via a brute force computer search.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

We study the following basic question on boolean functions:

How many
k
-SAT functions on
n
boolean variables are there?
What does a typical such function look like?

This question was first studied by Bollobás, Brightwell, and Leader
[
7
]
.
We focus on the regime where
k
is fixed and
n\to\infty
.
We will consider
k
-SAT functions in their disjunctive normal form (DNF). It would be an equivalent problem to enumerate
k
-SAT functions in their conjunctive normal form (CNF) since the negation of a DNF is a CNF and vice-versa.
For our purpose, a
k
-SAT function
on
n
boolean variables is a function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of the form

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=C_{1}\lor C_{2}\lor\cdots\lor C_{m},

where each
C_{i}
has the form
z_{1}\land\cdots\land z_{k}
with
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\in\{x_{1},\overline{x_{1}},\dots,x_{n},\overline{x_{n}}\}
.
Here we call
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
the
variables
.
Each of
x_{i}
and
\overline{x_{i}}
is a called a
literal
(
positive literal
and
negative literal
, respectively). Each
clause

C_{i}
is a conjunction (“and”) of
k
literals.
We further restrict that every clause uses
k
distinct variables (e.g., both
x_{1}\land x_{2}
and
x_{1}\land x_{2}\land\overline{x_{2}}
are invalid 3-SAT clauses).
This restriction does not lose any generality (the first example can be replaced by
(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land x_{3})\lor(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land\overline{x_{3}})
and the second example is a clause that is never satisfied and so can be deleted).
To simplify notation, we will drop the “and” symbol
\land
when writing a clause.
A
formula
is a set of clauses.
For instance, the
2
-SAT formula

(x_{1}\land x_{2})\lor(\overline{x_{1}}\land x_{3})\lor(x_{3}\land x_{4})
is written as

\{x_{1}x_{2},\overline{x_{1}}x_{3},x_{3}x_{4}\}
.
Every
k
-SAT function has a
k
-SAT formula, but different
k
-SAT formulae may correspond to the same
k
-SAT function.

Given the importance of
k
-SAT functions, it is a natural question to try to understand how rich this family of functions is.
While the total number of functions
\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
is
2^{2^{n}}
, the number of
k
-SAT functions is significantly smaller.
As an easy upper bound, since there are
2^{k}\binom{n}{k}
possible clauses, the number of
k
-SAT formulae is
2^{2^{k}\binom{n}{k}}
.
So the number of
k
-SAT functions is at most
2^{2^{k}\binom{n}{k}}
,
which is significantly smaller than
2^{2^{n}}
for a fixed
k
and large
n
.
The actual number of
k
-SAT functions turns out to be considerably smaller than even this upper bound.

A
k
-SAT formula is
monotone
if it only uses positive literals.
A
k
-SAT function is monotone if it has a monotone
k
-SAT formula.
There are
\binom{n}{k}
possible monotone clauses, and every monotone
k
-SAT formula produces a unique monotone
k
-SAT function, and so there are
2^{\binom{n}{k}}
monotone
k
-SAT functions.

A
k
-SAT function or formula is
unate
if it is monotone after replacing some variables with their negations (e.g.,
\{\overline{x_{1}}x_{2},\overline{x_{1}}x_{2},x_{2}x_{3}\}
is unate but
\{\overline{x_{1}}x_{2},x_{1}x_{2},x_{2}x_{3}\}
is not).
The number of unate
k
-SAT formulae that use all
n
variables is at least

2^{n}\left(2^{\binom{n}{k}}-n2^{\binom{n-1}{k}}\right)=(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}},

for fixed
k
as
n\to\infty
. Indeed, for each variable
x_{i}
, there are
2^{n}
choices as to whether to use it as a positive literal or a negative literal and there are at least
2^{\binom{n}{k}}-n2^{\binom{n-1}{k}}
monotone formulae that use all
n
variables.
All unate
k
-SAT formulae represent distinct functions, and thus the number of unate
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is at least
(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}}
.

Bollobás, Brightwell, and Leader
[
7
]
conjectured the following.

Conjecture 1.1
.

Fix
k\geq 2
.
The number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
boolean variables is
(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}}
.
Equivalently: a
1-o(1)
fraction of all
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables are unate.

Bollobás, Brightwell, and Leader also proposed a weaker version of this conjecture, namely that the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
boolean variables is
2^{(1+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
, and established this weaker conjecture for
k=2
.

1.1
was proved for
k=2
by Allen
[
1
]
and for
k=3
by Ilinca and Kahn
[
18
]
. The proofs in
[
7
,
1
]
for
k=2
used graph regularity (
[
17
]
gave an alternate regularity-free proof for
k=2
), whereas the proof for
k=3

[
18
]
used hypergraph regularity. Bollobás and Brightwell
[
6
]
further conjectured that even if
k=k(n)
is allowed to increase with
n
, as long as
k\leq(1/2-c)n
for some constant
c>0
, the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
-variables is
2^{(1+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
. In that paper
[
6
]
, they proved bounds on the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables in the regime
k\geq n/2
, where a completely different asymptotic behavior arises.

The goal of our paper here is two-fold. First, we reduce
1.1
for each
k
to a specific extremal problem about partially directed hypergraphs, analogous to classical hypergraph Turán density problems.
Hypergraph Turán density problems have been intensely studied, although only solved in a relatively small number of cases (see survey by Keevash
[
22
]
).
Our reduction is essentially lossless, as we show that the
k
-SAT enumeration problem (for fixed
k
) is equivalent to the corresponding Turán density problem.
Whereas previous approaches to enumerating
k\SAT
functions looked at specific forbidden structures (e.g., odd-blue-triangle-free graphs
[
7
,
1
,
17
]
), our approach is more systematic and identifies all relevant obstructions.
Our reduction uses the hypergraph container method, as opposed to the graph and hypergraph regularity methods used in earlier works.

The second goal of our paper is to prove
1.1
for
k=4
by solving the corresponding hypergraph Turán density problem,
1.25
below.
We also give an easier proof of the corresponding
k=3
problem compared to the methods in
[
18
]
.
For every fixed
k
, in principle one might be able to confirm the conjecture (after our reduction) via a finite computation, but the size of the computation grows extremely quickly (the approach in
[
18
]
for
k=3
essentially amounts to checking 5-vertex cases by hand).
To solve the
k=4
case of the problem, we apply a recent result of Füredi and Melaki
[
13
]
on the minimum number of triangular edges in a graph with a given number of vertices and edges.
In an appendix, we further confirm the
k=5
case via a brute force computer search.
To prove
1.1
for additional values of
k
(or ideally for all
k
), it remains to solve a Turán density type problem,
1.25
below.

1.2.
An extremal open problem

We state a tantalizing conjecture that would imply
1.1
.

A
partially directed graph
(also known as a
mixed graph
) is formed by taking a graph and orienting a subset of its edges (to orient an edge means to choose one of two directions for the edge), so every edge is either directed or undirected. An example is illustrated below.

1
2
3
4

Given a pair of partially directed graphs
\vec{H}
and
\vec{G}
, we say that
\vec{H}
is a
subgraph
of
\vec{G}
if one can obtain
\vec{H}
from
\vec{G}
by a combination of (1) removing vertices, (2) removing edges, and (3) removing the orientation of some edges.

The following partially directed graph plays a special role:

Below, the left graph contains
\vec{T}_{2}
as a subgraph, and the right does not contain
\vec{T}_{2}
as a subgraph.

The following statement implies
1.1
for
k=2
(see
Theorem

1.24
for the full statement of the implication).

Theorem 1.2
.

For all sufficiently large
n
,
every
n
-vertex partially directed graph with
\alpha\binom{n}{2}
undirected edges and
\beta\binom{n}{2}
directed edges and not containing
\vec{T}_{2}
as a subgraph satisfies

\alpha+(\log_{2}3)\beta\leq 1.

In fact, we know the optimal constant in front of
\beta
: it is
2-o(1)
(see
2.3
).
By orientating all the edges of
K_{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor,\left\lceil n/2\right\rceil}
from one part to the other part, we obtain a partially directed graph with
\left\lfloor n^{2}/4\right\rfloor
directed edges and no
\vec{T}_{2}
. This construction shows that one cannot do better than
\beta=2-o(1)
.

Now let us generalize the problem to hypergraphs.

A
partially directed 3-graph
(
3-PDG
) is formed by taking a 3-graph and orienting a some subset of edges. Here to
orient
an edge means to pick some vertex in the edge (we say that this is a
directed edge
that is
directed towards
or
pointed at
the chosen vertex). We notate a directed edge by putting a
\vee
on top of the pointed vertex, e.g.,
12\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.5pt\hbox{$3$}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt .

from earlier:

We define
subgraph
the same as earlier, i.e., obtainable by deleting vertices and edges as well as removing the orientation of some edges.

We prove the following result, which in turn (via
Theorem

1.24
) implies
1.1
for
k=3
.

Theorem 1.3
.

For all sufficiently large
n
,
every
n
-vertex 3-PDG with
\alpha\binom{n}{3}
undirected edges and
\beta\binom{n}{3}
directed edges and not containing
\vec{T}_{3}
as a subgraph satisfies

\alpha+(\log_{2}3)\beta\leq 1.

We can extend the above definitions to
partially directed
k
-graphs
(
k
-PDG
).
For each
k\geq 3
, we define
\vec{T}_{k}
, the
partially directed
k
-triangle
, as the
k
-PDG obtained by starting with
\vec{T}_{2}
and then adding
k-2
common vertices to all three edges, e.g.,

}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt},12356\}.

For each fixed
k
, the following conjecture implies
1.1
(via
Theorem

1.24
).

Conjecture 1.4
.

Fix
k\geq 2
.
For all sufficiently large
n
,
every
n
-vertex
k
-PDG with
\alpha\binom{n}{k}
undirected edges and
\beta\binom{n}{k}
directed edges and not containing
\vec{T}_{k}
as a subgraph satisfies

\alpha+(\log_{2}3)\beta\leq 1.

We also show that by enlarging the set of forbidden subgraphs from
\vec{T}_{k}
to some special finite set
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, the extremal claim
\alpha+(\log_{2}3)\beta\leq 1
becomes essentially equivalent to
1.1
. This set
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is constructed in
1.16
below.

For each
k
, if the statement is true for some
n
, then it is true for all larger
n
by a standard averaging argument.
So in principal, the conjecture could be verified via a finite computation.
However, this computation grows extremely quickly with
k
.

Section

2
contains our proof of the conjecture for
k\leq 4
via extremal graph theoretic techniques.
Appendix
A
contains a brute force solution to
k=5
.
The brute force method is unlikely to extend further, and so the extremal graph theoretic techniques may be valuable for future work.

1.3.
Strategy

Now let us describe our strategy for enumerating
k
-SAT functions.
Our setup is closest to the work of Ilinca and Kahn
[
18
]
where they enumerated
3
-SAT functions.

Definition 1.5

(Minimal formula)
.

A formula
G=\{C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{l}\}
on variables
\{x_{1},\ldots x_{n}\}
is
minimal
if deleting any clause from
G
changes the resulting boolean function.
Equivalently,
G
is minimal if for each clause
C_{i}\in G
, there is an assignment
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that satisfies
C_{i}
but no other
C_{j}\in G
,
j\neq i
. We call such
\mathsf{w}
(not necessarily unique) a
witness
to
C_{i}
.
1
1

1

In
[
18
]
, the term “non-redundant” is used for what we are calling “minimal.”

Example 1.6
.

The
2
-SAT formula
\{wx,wy,x\overline{z},\overline{y}z\}
is not minimal since it is impossible to satisfy only
wx
and no other clause.
Indeed, if we attempt to only satisfy
wx
, we must assign
w=1
and
x=1
;
to avoid satisfying
wy
and
x\overline{z}
, we must assign
y=0
and
z=1
, which would then lead to the final clause
\overline{y}z
being satisfied.

Every
k
-SAT function can be expressed as a minimal formula, but possibly in more than one way. We upper bound the number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae, which in turn upper bounds the number of
k
-SAT functions.

To bound the number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae, we identify a fixed set
\mathcal{B}
of non-minimal formulae, and then find an upper bound to the number of (not necessarily minimal) formulae not containing anything from
\mathcal{B}
as a subformulae.
We will show that, by setting
\mathcal{B}
to be all non-minimal formulae on a constant number of variables, the number of
\mathcal{B}
-free formulae is asymptotically the same as the number of minimal formulae.

Definition 1.7

(Subformula)
.

Given a formula
G
, a
subformula
of
G
is a subset of clauses of
G
.
We say that two formulae are
isomorphic
if one can be obtained from the other by relabeling variables.
Given another formula
F
, a
copy
of
F
in
G
is a subformula of
G
that is isomorphic to
F
.
Given a set
\mathcal{B}
of
k
-SAT formulae, we say that
G
is
\mathcal{B}
-free
if
G
has no copy of
B
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

The problem of counting
\mathcal{B}
-free formulae is analogous to counting
F
-free graphs on
n
vertices.
A classic result by Erdős, Kleitman, and Rothschild
[
11
]
shows that almost all triangle-free graphs are bipartite.
Erdős, Frankl, and Rödl
[
10
]
generalized this result and showed that for a fixed graph
F
, the number of
n
-vertex
F
-free graph is
2^{\operatorname{ex}(n,F)+o(n^{2})}
.
This result was initially proved using the graph regularity method.
It can also be proved as quick application of the more recently developed hypergraph container method
[
3
,
26
]
(see survey
[
4
]
).
Earlier works on
k
-SAT enumeration are closer in spirit to the regularity approach to counting
F
-free graphs.
Here we instead use the container method, which is simpler to carry out.

Here is a quick sketch of how to use the container method to enumerate
n
-vertex triangle-free graphs.
By an application of the hypergraph container theorem, there is a collection
\mathcal{G}
of
n
-vertex graphs (“containers”)
each with
(1/4+o(1))n^{2}
edges,
such that
\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert=2^{o(n^{2})}

and every
n
-vertex triangle-free graph is a subgraph of some container
G\in\mathcal{G}
.
It then follows that the number of
n
-vertex triangle-free graphs is at most
\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}2^{e(G)}=2^{(1/4+o(1))n^{2}}
.

As in the case of counting
F
-free graphs, the enumeration of
\mathcal{B}
-free formulae reduces to a certain extremal problem.
Here the situation diverges from the graph theory setting.

Definition 1.8

(Simple formula)
.

A
k
-SAT formula is
simple
if no two clauses use exactly the same set of
k
variables.

Example 1.9
.

The 3-SAT formula
\{wxy,wx\overline{z},xyz\}
is simple, whereas
\{xyz,xy\overline{z}\}
is not.

What happens when we apply a similar approach to enumerate
\mathcal{B}
-free formulae?
Every minimal
k
-SAT formula on
n
variables can be made simple by removing
o(n^{k})
clauses (
4.4
).
So let us focus on enumerating simple
\mathcal{B}
-free formulae.
The container theorem gives us a collection
\mathcal{G}
of
n
-variable “container” formulae with
\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert=2^{o(n^{k})}

such that every
n
-variable
\mathcal{B}
-free formula is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
.
Furthermore, every
G\in\mathcal{G}
is “not too large” in a certain sense.
For a container
G\in\mathcal{G}
, suppose
\alpha\binom{n}{k}

k
-subsets of variables support exactly one clause, and
\beta\binom{n}{k}

k
-subsets of variables support exactly two clauses.
It will always be the case that
o(n^{k})

k
-subsets of variables support more than two clauses.
Then the number of simple subformulae of
G
is
2^{\alpha\binom{n}{k}}3^{\beta\binom{n}{k}}2^{o(n^{k})}
, and we would like to guarantee that
\alpha+(\log_{2}3)\beta\leq 1+o(1)
.

Even if we wish to only enumerate simple formulae, we need to solve an extremal problem for non-simple formulae.
This is not an inadequacy of our approach, but actually an essential feature of the problem (see a related discussion at the end of
[
7
, Section 2]
).
This is where our situation differs from enumerating
F
-free graphs, which reduces to an extremal problem on
F
-free graphs instead of some more complicated object.

The extremal problem for formulae is rather unwieldly.
In earlier approaches, the idea is to isolate some specific patterns and analyze them by hand.
Here we take a different approach which is both simpler and more systematic.
We show that the extremal problem for formulae actually reduces to a Turán density type extremal problem on partially directed hypergraphs.
We explain what these objects are shortly in the next subsection.
We will define a concrete finite set
\mathcal{F}_{k}
of small partially directed
k
-graphs that turn out to completely capture the asymptotic enumeration of
k
-SAT functions.
This is a self-contained Turán density problem and can be studied independently of the
k
-SAT enumeration problem.
A solution to this Turán density problem would yield a resolution to
1.1
.
If the Turán density conjecture were false, then there would be way more
k
-SAT functions than in
1.1
.
Unfortunately, we were not able to resolve the Turán density conjecture in full, although we solve it for
k=4
, which is new.
Readers who are interested in working on the Turán problem only need to read the first two sections of this paper, as the rest of the paper concerns the reduction from the
k
-SAT enumeration problem to this Turán problem.

The container approach as described can only deduce an upper bound of the form
2^{(1+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
.
Obtaining the tighter asymptotic
(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}}
of
1.1
requires additional stability arguments.
By analogy, the container method sketched earlier shows that there are
2^{(1/4+o(1))n^{2}}
triangle-free graphs on
n
vertices.
To obtain the Erdős–Kleitman–Rothschild result in its full strength, namely that
1-o(1)
fraction of all triangle-free graphs are bipartite, one needs to apply additional stability arguments on top of the container method, as done in
[
2
]
(and later strengthened in
[
5
]
).

Recall that
1.1
is equivalent to saying that a
1-o(1)
fraction of all
k
-SAT functions are unate.
Far-from-unate formulae can be effectively handled by the container argument.
The final piece to show is that that almost all nearly unate formulae are unate.
We do this by extending the argument from
[
18
, Section 8]
, which only dealt with the
3
-SAT case;
our
k
-SAT argument has to overcome further technical challenges.

1.4.
Partially directed hypergraphs

Now we provide some precise definitions.
While there are many possible notions of a directed hypergraph, the relevant notion for us is the one where a directed edge is an edge with along with “pointed” vertex on the edge.
A partially directed hypergraph is formed from a hypergraph by directing some of its edges and leaving others intact.

Definition 1.10

(
k
-PDG)
.

A
partially directed
k
-graph
, abbreviated
k
-PDG
, is given by a set
V
of
n
vertices, and, for each unordered
k
-element subset
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
, one of the following possibilities:

(a)

No edge with vertices
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
, or

(b)

An
undirected edge
with vertices
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
, or

(c)

A
directed edge
using vertices
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
along with a choice of some
v_{i}\in\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
; we say that the edge is
directed
(or
pointed
) towards
v_{i}
. We notate such a directed edge by
v_{1}\cdots\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.603pt\hbox{$v$}\hskip-2.603pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}_{i}\cdots v_{k}
.

In particular, no two edges (whether directed or undirected) of a
k
-PDG can use the exact same set of
k
vertices.

Example 1.11
.

This is the edge set of a
3
-PDG:
\{123,12\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.5pt\hbox{$4$}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt \{123,12\}
(not 3-uniform).

Given a
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
, let
e_{u}(\vec{H})
be the number of undirected edges in
\vec{H}
and
e_{d}(\vec{H})
be the number of directed edges in
\vec{H}
. Denote their edge densities by

\alpha(\vec{H})=\frac{e_{u}(\vec{H})}{\binom{n}{k}}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta(\vec{H})=\frac{e_{d}(\vec{H})}{\binom{n}{k}}.

Definition 1.12

(Subgraph of
k
-PDG)
.

We say that a
k
-PDG
\vec{F}
is a
subgraph
of another
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
if we can obtain a
k
-PDG isomorphic to
\vec{F}
starting from
\vec{H}
by some sequence of actions of the following types: (a) deleting an edge, (b) deleting a vertex, and (c) forgetting the direction of an edge.

Definition 1.13

(
\mathcal{F}
-free
k
-PDG)
.

Given a set
\mathcal{F}
of
k
-PDGs, we say that a
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
is
\mathcal{F}
-free
if
\vec{H}
does not contain any element of
\mathcal{F}
as a subgraph.

We usually denote
k
-PDGs by simply writing their edge sets.

Definition 1.15

(Turán density for
k
-PDGs)
.

Given a set
\mathcal{F}
of
k
-PDGs, let
\pi(\mathcal{F},\theta)
be the smallest real number such that every
n
-vertex
\mathcal{F}
-free
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
satisfies

\alpha(\vec{H})+\theta\cdot\beta(\vec{H})\leq\pi(\mathcal{F},\theta)+o_{n\to\infty}(1).

We will consider the following finite family
\mathcal{F}_{k}
of forbidden structures, that will completely certify
minimality
of the
k
-SAT formula associated to a particular
k
-PDG.

As it turns out, our proof of
1.1
for
k=2,3,4
, and
5
, the Turán density problem only needs to forbid
\vec{T}_{k}
, which recall is the generalized triangle with one directed edge introduced in the previous subsection;
T_{k}
is also the first listed element (a) of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
in
Examples

1.20
,
1.21
and
1.22
.

Definition 1.16
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Let the family
\mathcal{F}_{k}
be the set of
k
-PDGs that can be built from the following procedure, and furthermore are non-redundant in the sense of not containing as a subgraph a different
k
-PDG arising from the same procedure.

(1)

Initialization.
Start with a single edge
e_{0}
that may be directed or undirected.
If
e_{0}
is undirected, then let
S
be its set of
k
vertices.
If
e_{0}
is directed, then let
S
be the set of
k-1
undirected vertices of
e_{0}
. Note that
S
is a set of unpointed vertices and it will maintain this property later.

(2)

Extensions.
Add
j
directed edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{j}
, with
0\leq j\leq k
, where each new edge
e_{i}
is directed towards some new vertex
w_{i}
and its
k-1
remaining vertices are chosen from
S
. Here
w_{1},\dots,w_{j}
are all distinct and outside
e_{0}
. Let
T
be the set of all
k+j
vertices seen so far.

(3)

Closure.
Either

(a)

add a new undirected edge contained in
T
(note that this can only happen if we had
j>0
in the previous step, as every
k
vertices are allowed to support at most one edge), or

(b)

add an edge directed towards some new vertex
w_{0}\notin T
with its remaining
k-1
vertices all contained in
T
and at least one of these
k-1
vertices outside
S
.

Call this final edge
e_{j+1}
.

Remark 1.17
.

It is perhaps unclear why the above forbidden structures are of interest. To give a brief glimpse, consider associating
\vec{T}_{4}\in\mathcal{F}_{4}
with the following
4
-SAT formula:

\phi_{\vec{T}_{4}}=(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land x_{3}\land x_{4})\lor(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land x_{3}\land\overline{x_{5}})\lor(x_{1}\land x_{2}\land x_{4}\land x_{5}).

Observe that
\phi_{\vec{T}_{4}}
is non-minimal, as satisfying the first clause by assigning
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=1
necessitates that one of the latter two clauses in
\phi_{\vec{T}_{4}}
is satisfied (which one depends on our assignment of
x_{5}
). Further, any
4
-SAT formula that contains a pattern like
\phi_{\vec{T}_{4}}
is also non-minimal. Thus, one can think of
\vec{T}_{4}
as a a representation of a “short certificate for non-minimality” (noting that non-minimal formula may still lack a
\vec{T}_{k}
-type pattern).

Remark 1.18
.

Let us emphasize again that some of the subgraphs generated by this procedure may include another as a subgraph, in which case we do not include the supergraph in
\mathcal{F}_{k}
. For example, both

can be generated by the procedure, but
F
is a subgraph of
F^{\prime}
and so we do not include
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}_{3}
; on the other hand,
F\in\mathcal{F}_{3}
since no subgraph of
F
other than itself can be generated by the procedure.

Remark 1.19

(Maximum size of an element of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
)
.

Every
k
-PDG in
\mathcal{F}_{k}
has at most
2k
vertices and at most
k+2
edges.
Indeed, by the end of the extension step, we can have up to
2k
vertices and
k+1
edges.
If the closure edge adds a new vertex, then one of the edges added in the extension much must have been redundant (see previous remark).

455.45732pt\hskip-2.5pt\hbox{$5$}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}

1.5.
Results

We will show how the enumeration of
k
-SAT functions is determined by the Turán density problem
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)
.
Note that for every
k
and
\theta
we always have

\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)\geq 1

since the complete undirected
k
-PDG is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free. (This is related to the fact that every monotone formula is minimal.)
Also, note the trivial inequality
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)\leq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta^{\prime})
if
\theta<\theta^{\prime}
.

Theorem 1.23
.

Fix
k\geq 2
.
The number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is

2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}.

In particular, for each
k\geq 2
, if
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)=1
(conjectured to be true, and we can prove it for
k\leq 4
), then the number of
k
-SAT functions is
2^{(1+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
, which corresponds to a weaker version of
1.1
. To get the full
1.1
, we just need a tiny bit more on the Turán density problem.

Theorem 1.24
.

Fix
k\geq 2
. If
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
, then the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is
(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}}
(i.e.,
1.1
is true for this
k
).

Thus, to prove
1.1
, we simply need to show the following.

Conjecture 1.25
.

For every
k\geq 2
, there is some constant
\theta>\log_{2}3
such that
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
.

We prove this conjecture for
k=2,3,4
, thereby yielding the following result.

Theorem 1.26
.

For
k=2,3,4
,
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
.
Consequently, the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is
(1+o(1))2^{n+\binom{n}{k}}
.

Remark 1.27
.

If one could prove
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)=1
then almost certainly the same proof verbatim would also yield
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
.
Indeed, the number
\log_{2}3
plays no special role for the Turán density problem and is only meaningful because of its applications for
k
-SAT enumeration.
So our results show that the
k
-SAT enumeration problem is essentially equivalent to determining
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)
.

Remark 1.28
.

For any fixed
k
,
1.25
, if true, is verifiable via a finite computation, namely by checking over all
n_{k}
-vertex
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free
k
-PDGs for some sufficiently large but finite
n_{k}
(depending on
\theta
).
In
[
18
]
, an extremal problem of similar nature for
k=3
was checked “by hand” over
5
-vertex graphs.
We do not take the brute-force computational approach for Theorem
1.26
.
Instead, we solve the
k=4
problem using extremal graph theoretic arguments, with the help of a recent result of Füredi and Maleki
[
13
]
.
We also give a much simpler argument for
k=3
, which does not involve any case checking.

In Appendix
A
, we include a description of a C++ implementation of an algorithm that does a brute force check to find that for all
7
-vertex
4
-PDGs,
\pi(\vec{T}_{k},7/4)=1
. This, in concert with Lemma
2.1
, verifies Conjecture
1.25
for
k=5
.

For larger values of
k
, previously
[
7
, Theorem 5.3]
showed that the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is at most
2^{\sqrt{(k+1)\pi}\binom{n}{k}}
for all
n\geq 2k
.
We obtain the following improved bound, shy of proving of
1.25
.

Theorem 1.29
.

For all fixed
k\geq 5
we have

\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)\leq\frac{\log_{2}3}{2.727/k+1}.

Consequently, the number of
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is

2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}\leq 2^{\left(\frac{\log_{2}3}{2.727/k+1}+o(1)\right)\binom{n}{k}}.

1.6.
Outline

The rest of the paper can be divided into three parts.

Part I. A Turán density problem.

In
Section

2
, we study the Turán density problem and prove
1.25
for
k=2,3,4
. The readers who are interested in working on
1.1
only need to solve the remaining Turán density problem and thus do not need to read beyond this section.

Part II. Exponential asymptotics.

The goal of this part is to prove
Theorem

1.23
.
In
Section

3
, we recall some tools for hypergraphs, including the hypergraph container theorem.
In
Section

4
, we prove an upper bound on the number of
k
-SAT functions.
In
Section

5
, we prove a matching lower bound.

Part III. A stability argument.

In the last part of this article, we prove
Theorem

1.24
.
In
Section

6
, by a more careful analysis of the containers, we reduce the problem to showing there are a negligibly many non-unate but close-to-unate minimal formulae.
This final claim is then established in
Section

7
by extending the arguments in
[
18
, Section 8]
.

2.
A Turán density problem

The general hypergraph Turán problem seeks to answer the following extremal question: how many edges can an
n
-vertex
k
-uniform hypergraph have without containing a copy of forbidden
k
-uniform hypergraph (“
k
-graph”)
F
?
Many methods for tackling such problems are described in
[
22
]
.
We consider a variant of the classical hypergraph Turán problem and work with
k
-PDGs as defined in the introduction.
The main objective here is to show
1.25
, that for each
k\geq 2
,
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
; we prove this conjecture for
k=2,3,4
here (and also
k=5
in the appanedix), and leave all larger values of
k
as an open problem.
Towards the conjecture, it suffices to prove

\pi(\vec{T}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3

where
\vec{T}_{k}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
is the special
k
-PDG defined in
Section

1.2
(since
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free implies
\vec{T}_{k}
-free).

2.1.
Results

We prove the following recursive bound relating the Turán problem associated to
k
-SAT with that of
(k-1)
-SAT.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
k\geq 3
, we have that

\pi\left(\vec{T}_{k},\frac{(k-1)\theta+1}{k}\right)\leq\pi(\vec{T}_{k-1},\theta).

Remark 2.2
.

The same proof also shows the inequality

\pi\left(\mathcal{F}_{k},\frac{(k-1)\theta+1}{k}\right)\leq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\theta).

Recall that since the complete undirected
k
-PDG on
n
vertices is
\vec{T}_{k}
-free,
\pi(\vec{T}_{k},\theta)\geq 1
for all
\theta>0
.
The following result solves the Turán problem for enumerating
2
-SAT and
3
-SAT functions (
5/3>\log_{2}3=1.58496\dots
).
We will see its short proof momentarily.
The second claim follows from the first by
2.2
.

Proposition 2.3
.

\pi(\vec{T}_{2},2)=1
. Therefore,
\pi(\vec{T}_{3},5/3)=1
.

The result for
4
-SAT follows from the following improved bound on
\pi(\vec{T}_{3},\theta)
.
The second claim again follows from the first by
Lemma

2.1
.

Proposition 2.4
.

\pi(\vec{T}_{3},\phi)=1
with
\phi=1.909>(4\log_{2}3-1)/3
. Consequently,
\pi(\vec{T}_{4},\theta)=1
for
\theta=(3\phi+1)/4>\log_{2}3
.

Remark 2.5
.

To prove
1.25
for
k=5
,
by applying
Lemma

2.1
twice,
it suffices to show
\pi(\vec{T}_{3},\phi)=1
for some
\phi>1.975>(5\log_{2}3-2)/3
.

2.3
and
2.4
together lead to
Theorem

1.26
. To obtain an upper bound on
\pi(\vec{T}_{k},\phi)
for larger
k
,
iterating
Lemma

2.1
gives

\pi\left(\vec{T}_{k},\frac{\ell}{k}(\phi-1)+1\right)\leq\pi(\vec{T}_{\ell},\phi)\qquad\text{for }k>\ell\geq 2.

Using
\ell=3
and
\phi=1.909
, we get

\pi(\vec{T}_{k},3(\phi-1)/k+1)=1
for all
k\geq 4
.
Since
3(\phi-1)/k+1<\log_{2}3
for all
k\geq 5
, we have

\pi(\vec{T}_{k},\log_{2}3)\leq\frac{\log_{2}3}{3(\phi-1)/k+1}\cdot\pi\left(\vec{T}_{k},\frac{3}{k}(\phi-1){k}+1\right)\leq\frac{\log_{2}3}{2.727/k+1}\qquad\text{for all }k\geq 5.

This gives
Theorem

1.29
.
Our work leads to the following natural Turán problem for
k
-PDGs.

Problem 2.6
.

For each
k\geq 2
, what is the largest
\theta
such that
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
?

The following
k
-PDG is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free:
partitioning the
n
vertices as
A\cup B
with
\left\lvert A\right\rvert=n/k
and
\left\lvert B\right\rvert=(k-1)n/k
, and add all directed edges with
k-1
vertices in
B
and directed towards a vertex in
A
.
This
k
-PDG has
\left\lvert A\right\rvert\binom{\left\lvert B\right\rvert}{k-1}=(n/k)\binom{(1-1/k)n}{k-1}=(1+o(1))(1-1/k)^{k-1}\binom{n}{k}
edges. So
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)>1
whenever
\theta>(1-1/k)^{-k+1}
.
We conjecture that this construction is optimal in the following sense.

Conjecture 2.7
.

\pi(\vec{T}_{k},(1-1/k)^{-k+1})=1
for every
k\geq 2
.

2.3
confirms this conjecture for
k=2
.
The conjectured optimal construction is reminiscent of the solution to the hypergraph Turán problem corresponding to 3-graphs with independent neighborhoods by Füredi, Pikhurko, and Simonovits
[
14
]
.
Our techniques are partly inspired by their work.

The following conjecture is a restatement of
1.4
, which would imply
1.25
and hence
1.1
.
In this section, we prove it for
k\leq 4
, and in the appendix we prove it for
k\leq 5
.

Conjecture 2.8
.

For every
k\geq 2
,
\pi(\vec{T}_{k},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
.

2.2.
An averaging argument

We first prove
Lemma

2.1
via an averaging argument.

Definition 2.9
.

Given a
k
-PDG
\vec{H}=(V,E)
and a vertex
v\in V
, the
link of
v
, denoted
\vec{H}_{v}
, is the
(k-1)
-PDG with vertex set
V\setminus\{v\}
and with edges obtained by taking all edges of
\vec{H}
containing
v
and then deleting the vertex
v
from each edge. We preserve all the direction data, except that every edge directed towards
v
in
\vec{H}
becomes an undirected edge in
\vec{H}_{v}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

If
\vec{H}
is
\vec{T}_{k}
-free, then
\vec{H}_{v}
is
\vec{T}_{k-1}
-free for all
v\in V(\vec{H})
.

Proof.

If, for some vertex
v
,
\vec{H}_{v}
contains
\vec{T}_{k-1}
as a subgraph, then adding
v
to all the edges shows that
\vec{H}
contains
\vec{T}_{k}
as a subgraph.
∎

In the proofs below, we drop dependencies on
\vec{H}
from notation when there is no confusion:

\alpha=\alpha(\vec{H}),\quad\alpha_{v}=\alpha(\vec{H}_{v}),\quad\beta=\beta(\vec{H}),\quad\beta_{v}=\beta(\vec{H}_{v}).

Proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

Let
\vec{H}
be a
\vec{T}_{k}
-free
k
-PDG.
By linearity of expectation over a uniform random vertex
v\in V
,
\mathbb{E}_{v}\alpha_{v}=\alpha+\beta/k
and

\mathbb{E}_{v}\beta_{v}=(k-1)\beta/k
.
For every
v\in V
,
\vec{H}_{v}
is
\vec{T}_{k-1}
-free, so
\alpha_{v}+\theta\cdot\beta_{v}\leq\pi(\vec{T}_{k-1},\theta)+o(1)
. Thus, we have that

\alpha+\frac{\theta(k-1)+1}{k}\cdot\beta\leq\mathbb{E}_{v}[\alpha_{v}+\theta\cdot\beta_{v}]\leq\pi(\vec{T}_{k-1},\theta)+o(1).

This implies
\pi\left(\vec{T}_{k},\frac{(k-1)\theta+1}{k}\right)\leq\pi(\vec{T}_{k-1},\theta)
.
∎

2.3.
Turán density for 2-PDGs

We leverage the following lemma of Füredi to show
2.3
, that partially directed graphs
\vec{H}
satisfy
\pi(\vec{T}_{2},2)=1
.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
12
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
, let
G^{2}
be the graph on vertex set
V
with
xy\in E(G^{2})
if and only if there is some
z\in V
with
xz,yz\in E
. Then, for any graph
G
,

e(G^{2})\geq e(G)-\lfloor n/2\rfloor.

In a graph
G
, we say that edge
xy\in E(G)
is a
triangular edge
if there exists some
z\in V(G)
so that
xz,yz\in E(G)
.
We then have the following corollary, showing that
\pi(\vec{T}_{2},2)=1
, which completes the proof of
2.3
.

Corollary 2.12
.

If a 2-PDG
\vec{H}
does not contain
\vec{T}_{2}
as a subgraph, then
\alpha+2\beta\leq 1+o(1)
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the underlying simple graph of
\vec{H}
(the graph obtained from
\vec{H}
by replacing directed edges with simple edges).
Then
e(G)=(\alpha+\beta){n\choose 2}
.
Only non-triangular edges in
G
can arise as directed edges in
\vec{H}
. We further note that all triangular edges in
G
are in
G^{2}
. Thus by
Lemma

2.11
,

{n\choose 2}\geq e(G^{2})+\beta{n\choose 2}\geq e(G)+\beta{n\choose 2}-O(n)=(\alpha+2\beta){n\choose 2}-O(n),

which implies that
\alpha+2\beta\leq 1+o(1)
.
∎

2.4.
An improvement for 3-PDGs

Here we prove
2.4
that
\pi(\vec{T}_{3},1.909)=1
.
We begin with a more careful decomposition of the links, followed by an averaging-inductive argument.

Definition 2.13
.

For a
3
-PDG
\vec{H}=(V,E)
and a vertex
v\in V
, we define four associated link (di)graphs. Let
A_{v}
be a graph with edges arising from undirected edges in
\vec{H}
containing
v
, i.e.,

A_{v}=\left(V\backslash\{v\},\{w_{1}w_{2}:vw_{1}w_{2}\in E\}\right).

Let
a_{v}:=e(A_{v})/{n-1\choose 2}
.
Let
B_{v}
be a graph arising from edges in
\vec{H}
containing but not directed towards
v
, i.e.,

B_{v}=\left(V\backslash\{v\},\{w_{1}w_{2}:vw_{1}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-3.71413pt\hbox{$w$}\hskip-3.71413pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}_{2}\in E\}\right).

Let
b_{v}:=e(B_{v})/{n-1\choose 2}
.
Let
C_{v}
be a graph arising from directed edges in
\vec{H}
pointed at
v
, i.e.,

C_{v}=\left(V\backslash\{v\},\{w_{1}w_{2}:\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.603pt\hbox{$v$}\hskip-2.603pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}w_{1}w_{2}\in E\}\right).

Let
c_{v}:=e(C_{v})/{n-1\choose 2}
.
So
B_{v}
is comprised of the directed edges of the link
\vec{H}_{v}
, whereas
A_{v}\cup C_{v}
is comprised of the subgraph consisting of undirected edges of
\vec{H}_{v}
.

Finally, we write
H_{v}
for the undirected graph formed by forgetting the directions in
\vec{H}_{v}
, i.e., the union of
A_{v}
,
B_{v}
, and
C_{v}
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\phi=1.909
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, there is some
n_{0}(\epsilon)
such that for every
\vec{T}_{3}
-free
3
-PDG
\vec{H}
on
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon)
vertices, every vertex
v
satisfies

a_{v}+\frac{3\phi-1}{2}\cdot b_{v}+c_{v}\leq 1+\epsilon\qquad\text{or}\qquad a_{v}+\phi\cdot(b_{v}+c_{v})\leq 1+\epsilon.

Proof of
2.4
using
2.14
.

It suffices to show that, for every fixed
\epsilon>0
, there is some
C=C(\epsilon)
so that every
n
-vertex
\vec{T}_{3}
-free
3
-PDG
\vec{H}
satisfies

(\alpha+\phi\cdot\beta)\binom{n}{k}=e_{u}+\phi\cdot e_{d}\leq(1+\epsilon)\binom{n}{k}+C

By choosing a large constant
C
, we may assume that the inequality holds for all
n<n_{0}(\epsilon)
.
So assume
n\geq n_{0}(\epsilon)
.
By
2.14
, every vertex
v
of
H
satisfies

a_{v}+\frac{3\phi-1}{2}\cdot b_{v}+c_{v}\leq 1+\epsilon\qquad\text{or}\qquad a_{v}+\phi\cdot(b_{v}+c_{v})\leq 1+\epsilon.

By linearity of expectation, we have

\mathbb{E}_{v}a_{v}=\alpha,\quad\mathbb{E}_{v}b_{v}=\frac{2\beta}{3},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{v}c_{v}=\frac{\beta}{3}.

Thus, if the first inequality in the statement is true for all
v
, then averaging over all
v
yields
\alpha+\phi\cdot\beta\leq 1+\epsilon
, as desired.

Now suppose that there is some
v
with
a_{v}+\phi\cdot(b_{v}+c_{v})\leq 1+\epsilon
.
The
(n-1)
-vertex
3
-PDG
\vec{H}-v
is also
\vec{T}_{3}
-free, so by the induction hypothesis,

e_{u}(\vec{H}-v)+\phi\cdot e_{d}(\vec{H}-v)\leq(1+\epsilon)\binom{n-1}{k}+C.

By separately considering edges in
\vec{H}
containing
v
and not containing
v
, we obtain

e_{u}(\vec{H})=a_{v}(\vec{H})\binom{n}{k-1}+e_{u}(\vec{H}-v)

and

e_{d}(\vec{H})=(b_{v}(\vec{H})+c_{v}(\vec{H}))\binom{n}{k-1}+e_{d}(\vec{H}-v).

Consequently,

\displaystyle e_{u}(\vec{H})+\phi\cdot e_{d}(\vec{H})

\displaystyle\leq(a_{v}(\vec{H})+\phi\cdot(b_{v}(\vec{H})+c_{v}(\vec{H})))\binom{n}{k-1}+(1+\epsilon)\binom{n-1}{k}+C

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\binom{n}{k-1}+(1+\epsilon)\binom{n-1}{k}+C

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\binom{n}{k}+C.

This completes the induction.
∎

Thus, it remains to show
2.14
.

Since
\vec{T}_{3}=\{vxy,vx\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.54515pt\hbox{$z$}\hskip-2.54515pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt},vyz\}
is forbidden, we see that every edge of
B_{v}
is non-triangular in the graph
H_{v}
.
This leads to the following natural question. What is the maximum possible density of non-triangular edges in a graph of given edge density (over 1/2)?
Fortunately, this problem was recently solved by Füredi and Maleki
[
13
]
.
An optimal construction comes from partitioning the vertex set into
A\cup B\cup C
and adding all edges within
A
as well as edges between
A\cup C
and
B
.
In this construction, the non-triangular edges are those between
B
and
C
.
One should optimize over possible sizes of the partition
A\cup B\cup C
to maximize the number of non-triangular edges while maintaining the required total number of edges (that this number is optimal is proved asymptotically in
[
13
]
and exactly in
[
15
]
for sufficiently large graphs).
The precise statement that we will use is given below, where the the function
f(\rho)
below is best possible due to the construction just described.

Theorem 2.15

(Füredi and Maleki
[
13
]
)
.

Fix
0\leq\rho\leq 1
. In an
n
-vertex graph with at least
\rho\binom{n}{2}
edges, the number of non-triangular edges is at most

(f(\rho)+o(1))\binom{n}{2},

where

(2.1)

f(\rho)=\max\left\{2yz:x+y+z=1,x^{2}+2xy+2yz\geq\rho,\ x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\right\}.

In contrast, the easier
Lemma

2.11
amounts to the inequality
f(\rho)\leq 1-\rho
, whereas
f(\rho)
is strictly convex for
1/2\leq\rho\leq 1
with
f(1/2)=1/2
and
f(1)=0
.
Here the strict convexity of
f
is crucial for our proof.

Proof of
2.14
.

Suppose that for some vertex
v
of
\vec{H}
, one has both

a_{v}+\frac{3\phi-1}{2}\cdot b_{v}+c_{v}>1+\epsilon\qquad\text{and}\qquad a_{v}+\phi\cdot(b_{v}+c_{v})>1+\epsilon.

Since every edge of
B_{v}
is non-triangular in
H_{v}
, by
Theorem

2.15
,

b_{v}\leq f(a_{v}+b_{v}+c_{v})+o(1).

It follows, provided
n
is sufficiently large, there exist real numbers
x,y,z,a,b,c
satisfying the following system (to get to this system, we set
a=a_{v}
,
b=\min\{b_{v},f(a_{v}+b_{v}+c_{v})\}
, and
c=c_{v}
, so that
b>b_{v}-o(1)
, which implies the first two inequalities as
1+\epsilon-o(1)
is strictly larger than
1
if
n
is sufficiently large):

\displaystyle a+\frac{3\phi-1}{2}\cdot b+c

\displaystyle>1,

\displaystyle a+\phi\cdot(b+c)

\displaystyle>1,

\displaystyle x+y+z

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle x^{2}+2xy+2yz

\displaystyle\geq a+b+c,

\displaystyle 2yz

\displaystyle\geq b,

\displaystyle a,b,c,x,y,z

\displaystyle\geq 0.

The following
Mathematica
code outputs to
False
when executed (under a second on a modern computer).
It proves that the above system has no real solutions for
\phi=1.909
, thereby yielding the desired contradiction.
This computer verification is rigorous and uses Collins’ cylindrical algebraic decomposition
[
9
]
(a form of quantifier elimination). See
[
21
]
for a tutorial.

phi = 1909/1000;
CylindricalDecomposition[
a + b (3 phi - 1)/2 + c > 1 &&
a + phi (b + c) > 1 &&
x + y + z == 1 &&
x^2 + 2 x y + 2 y z >= a + b + c &&
2 y z >= b &&
a >= 0 && b >= 0 && c >= 0 && x >= 0 && y >= 0 && z >= 0,
{x, y, z, a, b, c}]

∎

3.
Hypergraph Tools

We recall several standard tools on hypergraphs that will prove useful in our analysis of minimal
k
-SAT formulae and
k
-PDGs.

3.1.
Densities of blowups

Given a
k
-graph
H
, we write
H[b]
for the
b
-blowup
of
H
, defined to be the
k
-graph obtained by replacing every
v\in V(H)
with
b
duplicates, where
k
vertices in
V(H[b])
form an edge if and only if the vertices they originate from form an edge in
H
.

By a standard Cauchy–Schwarz argument, we know that if some
n
-vertex
k
-graph contains
\delta n^{\left\lvert V(H)\right\rvert}
homomorphic copies of
H
, then it contains at least
\delta n^{2\left\lvert V(H)\right\rvert}
homomorphic copies of
H[2]
.
For sufficiently large
n
, all but
O_{H}(n^{2\left\lvert V(H)\right\rvert-1})
such homomorphic copies of
H[2]
are actual subgraphs. So we have the following (here “copies” refers to isomorphic copies as subgraphs).

Lemma 3.1
.

For every
\epsilon>0
and
k
-graph
H
, there is some
\delta>0
and
n_{0}
such that for all
n>n_{0}
, if an
n
-vertex
k
-graph has at least
\epsilon n^{\left\lvert V(H)\right\rvert}
copies of
H
then it has at least
\delta n^{2\left\lvert V(H)\right\rvert}
copies of
H[2]
.

Given a
k
-SAT formula
G
, its
b
-blowup
, denoted
G[b]
, is obtained from
G
by replacing each variable by
b
identical duplicates. For example, for the
3
-SAT formula
G=\{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3},x_{1}\overline{x}_{3}x_{4}\}
, we have

G[2]=\left\{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3},x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime},x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3},x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}x_{3},x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}x_{3}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3},x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime},\right.\\
\left.x_{1}\overline{x_{3}}x_{4},x_{1}\overline{x_{3}}x_{4}^{\prime},x_{1}\overline{x_{3}^{\prime}}x_{4},x_{1}\overline{x_{3}^{\prime}}x_{4}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}\overline{x_{3}}x_{4},x_{1}^{\prime}\overline{x_{3}}x_{4}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}\overline{x_{3}^{\prime}}x_{4},x_{1}^{\prime}\overline{x_{3}^{\prime}}x_{4}^{\prime}\right\}

We have a similar conclusion for blowups of formulae.

Lemma 3.2
.

For every
\epsilon>0
and
k
-SAT formula
G
, there is some
\delta>0
and
n_{0}
such that for all
n>n_{0}
, if a
k
-SAT formula on
n
variables has at least
\epsilon n^{v(G)}
copies of
G
, then it has at least
\delta n^{2v(G)}
copies of
G[2]
.

3.2.
Kruskal–Katona theorem

We need the following special case of the Kruskal–Katona theorem
[
23
,
20
]
.
Here a
simplex
in a
k
-graph is a clique on
k+1
vertices.

Theorem 3.3
.

A
k
-uniform hypergraph on
n
-vertices with at most
\beta n^{k}/k!
edges contains at most
\beta^{\frac{k+1}{k}}n^{k+1}/(k+1)!
simplices.

Here is a quick proof of this statement using Shearer’s entropy inequality
[
8
]
(see
[
19
]
).

Proof.

Let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{k+1})
be the vertices of a uniformly chosen simplex in the
k
-graph, with the
k+1
vertices permutated uniformly at random.
By Shearer’s inequality, letting
\vec{X}_{-i}
denote
(X_{1},\dots,X_{k+1})
with the
i
-th coordinate removed, we have

\displaystyle k\log((k+1)!\text{\#simplices})

\displaystyle=kH(X_{1},\dots,X_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq H(\vec{X}_{-1})+\cdots+H(\vec{X}_{-(k+1)})

\displaystyle=(k+1)H(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})\leq(k+1)\log(k!\text{\#edges}).

Since
k!\text{\#edges}\leq\beta n^{k}
, we have
(k+1)!\text{\#simplices}\leq\beta^{\frac{k+1}{k}}n^{k+1}
.
∎

3.3.
Hypergraph containers

For any formula
B
, we let
v(B)
denote the number of variables that appear in some clause in
B
. For a finite set
\mathcal{B}
of
k
-SAT formulae, we let

m(\mathcal{B})=\max_{B\in\mathcal{B}}|B|.

We use the hypergraph container theorem, proved independently by
Balogh, Morris, and Samotij
[
3
]

and
Saxton and Thomason
[
26
]

to show the following.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finite set of simple
k
-SAT formulae.
For every
\delta>0
, there exists
C=C(\mathcal{B},\delta)
such that for every
n
, there exists a collection
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)
of formulae on variable set
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
such that:

(a)

Every
\mathcal{B}
-free formula with variables in
X
is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
, and

(b)

For every
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
G
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
, and

(c)

\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert\leq n^{Cn^{k-1/(m(\mathcal{B})-1)}}
.

In the remainder of this section, we deduce the above claim from the more general hypergraph container theorem. To state the general result, we introduce some notation for an
\ell
-uniform hypergraph
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})
denote the collection of independent vertex sets in
\mathcal{H}
. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, let
\Delta_{j}(\mathcal{H})
denote the maximum
j
-codegree of vertices in
\mathcal{H}
, i.e., the maximum number of edges containing a given
j
-vertex subset in
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathcal{P}(V)
denote the family of all subsets of
V
, and let
{V\choose\leq s}
denote the family of those subsets of
V
having size at most
s
.

For an increasing family (i.e., closed under taking supersets) of vertex sets
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{P}(V(\mathcal{H}))
, we say that
\mathcal{H}
is
(\mathcal{F},\epsilon)
-dense if
e(\mathcal{H}[A])\geq\epsilon e(\mathcal{H})
for every
A\in\mathcal{F}
. A simple example of
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{P}(V(\mathcal{H}))
for which
\mathcal{H}
is
(\mathcal{F},\epsilon)
-dense is the family

\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}=\{A\subset V(\mathcal{H}):e(\mathcal{H}[A])\geq\epsilon e(\mathcal{H})\}.

We apply the following version of the
hypergraph container theorem as stated in
[
3
, Theorem 2.2]
,
A similar result was proved in
[
26
]
.

Theorem 3.5

(Hypergraph container theorem)
.

For every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
c,\epsilon>0
, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\ell,c,\epsilon)>0
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{H}
be an
\ell
-uniform hypergraph and
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{P}(V(\mathcal{H}))
be an increasing family of sets such that
|A|\geq\epsilon v(\mathcal{H})
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
is
(\mathcal{F},\epsilon)
-dense and there exists
p\in(0,1)
such that, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, we have

\Delta_{j}(\mathcal{H})\leq c\cdot p^{j-1}\cdot\frac{e(\mathcal{H})}{v(\mathcal{H})}.

Then there exists a family
\mathcal{S}\subset{V(\mathcal{H})\choose\leq C_{0}\cdot p\cdot v(\mathcal{H})}
and functions
f:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{F}}
,
g:\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
such that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})
, we have:

(i)

g(I)\subset I
;

(ii)

I\setminus g(I)\subset f(g(I))
.

To prove
Theorem

3.4
using
Theorem

3.5
, we
proceed in two steps. First, we enlarge
\mathcal{B}
to a set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of formulae, each with
m(\mathcal{B})
clauses. This allows us to construct an
m(\mathcal{B})
-uniform hypergraph
\mathcal{H}_{k}
on the set of all clauses on
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, and apply the container argument. We then verify the codegree bounds of
Theorem

3.5
, which relies on all the elements of
\mathcal{B}
being simple.

We use
Theorem

3.5
to show that for every
\mathcal{B}
and
\delta>0
, we can take family
\mathcal{G}
of at most
n^{Cn^{k-1/(m(\mathcal{B})-1)}}
formulae on
X
, such that: (1) every
n
-vertex
\mathcal{B}
-free formula on
X
is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
; (2) for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, every
G\in\mathcal{G}
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
using
Theorem

3.5
.

Fix a finite set
\mathcal{B}
of simple
k
-SAT formulae. We set
m=m(\mathcal{B})
,
v=km
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
to be the set of all isomorphism classes of formulae
B^{\prime}
having the following properties:

•

B^{\prime}
has exactly
m
clauses;

•

B^{\prime}
has a variable set of size
v
; here we allow isolated variables (i.e., variables not used by any clause in
B^{\prime}
);

•

B^{\prime}
is simple;

•

there exists some
B\in\mathcal{B}
such that
B^{\prime}
has a copy of
B
.

Notice that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{B}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{k}=(V,E(\mathcal{H}_{k}))
be an
m
-uniform hypergraph, where
V
is the set of all
k
-SAT clauses on
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
(so
|V|=2^{k}{n\choose k}
), and

E(\mathcal{H}_{k})=\{G\subset V\mid G\text{ is isomorphic to some }B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}.

Every
k\SAT
formula
G
on
X
can be viewed as a vertex subset of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. For any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, if
G
has more than
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
, then there exists some
\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(B,\delta)>0
and
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
such that
G
has more than
\eta_{0}n^{v}
copies of
B^{\prime}
, with the property that
B^{\prime}
has a copy of
B
. Since
\mathcal{B}
is finite, we can take

\eta=\min_{B\in\mathcal{B}}\eta_{0}(B,\delta)>0.

This implies that there exists
\eta=\eta(\mathcal{B},\delta)>0
such that if
G
has more than
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, then
|E(\mathcal{H}_{k}[G])|>\eta n^{v}
.

Since every formula
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
has variable set of size
v
, for a formula
G\subset V
, the number of formulae
G^{\prime}\subset V
such that (1)
G\subset G^{\prime}
and (2) there exists some
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
with
G^{\prime}
isomorphic to
B^{\prime}
is
O_{\mathcal{B}}(n^{v-v(G)})
.
In particular, taking
G
to be the empty formula, we have
e(\mathcal{H}_{k})=O_{\mathcal{B}}(n^{v})
.

We therefore have the following upper bounds on the maximum
j
-codegrees of vertices in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. When
j=1
, since every single-clause formula
G=\{C\}
has
v(G)=k
, we have
\Delta_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{k})=O_{\mathcal{B}}(n^{v-k})
.
For all
j\in\{2,\dots,m\}
, since
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a set of simple formulae, and any simple formula
G=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{j}\}
with
j
clauses has
v(G)\geq k+1
, we have
\Delta_{j}(\mathcal{H}_{k})=O_{\mathcal{B}}(n^{v-k-1})
.

Let
p=n^{-1/(m-1)}
. Using a trivial lower bound
e(\mathcal{H}_{k})\geq{n\choose v}
(say) and the fact that
|V|=2^{k}{n\choose k}
, we can choose some
c=c(\mathcal{B})>0
such that

\Delta_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{k})\leq c\cdot\frac{{n\choose v}}{2^{k}{n\choose k}}\leq c\cdot\frac{e(\mathcal{H}_{k})}{|V|}

and

\Delta_{j}(\mathcal{H}_{k})\leq c\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{{n\choose v}}{2^{k}{n\choose k}}\leq c\cdot p^{j-1}\cdot\frac{e(\mathcal{H}_{k})}{|V|}

for all
j\in\{2,\dots,m\}
.

For any
\delta>0
, we set
\eta=\eta(\mathcal{B},\delta)>0
as above,
\epsilon=\epsilon(\mathcal{B},\delta)>0
sufficiently small, and

\mathcal{F}=\{A\subset V(\mathcal{H}):|A|\geq\epsilon\cdot v(\mathcal{H}_{k})\text{ and }e(\mathcal{H}_{k}[A])\geq\epsilon\cdot e(\mathcal{H}_{k})\},

so that
\mathcal{F}
is an increasing family of sets such that
|A|\geq\epsilon v(\mathcal{H}_{k})
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is
(\mathcal{F},\epsilon)
-dense.

Applying
Theorem

3.5
, we know that there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(m,c,\epsilon)=C_{0}(\mathcal{B},\delta)
, a family

\mathcal{S}\subset{V\choose\leq C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|}

and functions
f:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{F}}
,
g:\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
such that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H})
, we have:

(i)

g(I)\subset I
;

(ii)

I\setminus g(I)\subset f(g(I))
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)=\{S\cup f(S):S\in\mathcal{S}\}
. We show that
\mathcal{G}
meets all the conditions decribed in
Theorem

3.4
.

(a)
Every
\mathcal{B}
-free formula with
n
variables is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
.

Since every
\mathcal{B}
-free formula is clearly
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
-free, it is an independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{H}_{k})
. Since
I\setminus g(I)\subset f(g(I))
and
g(I)\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
I\subset(g(I)\cup f(g(I)))\in\mathcal{G}
.

(b)
For every
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
G
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
.

Every
G\in\mathcal{G}
is of the form
G=S\cup f(S)
for some
S\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
|S|\leq C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|
, the number of edges incident to
S\setminus f(S)
is at most

C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|\cdot\Delta_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{k})\leq C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot 2^{k}{n\choose k}\cdot\Delta_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{k})=O_{\mathcal{B},\delta}\left(n^{v-1/(m-1)}\right).

Meanwhile, since
f(S)\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}
, we either have
e(\mathcal{H}_{k}[f(S)])<\epsilon e(\mathcal{H}_{k})\leq\epsilon|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\cdot{n\choose v}\cdot(v!)
or have
|f(S)|<\epsilon v(\mathcal{H})
, in which case
e(\mathcal{H}_{k}[f(S)])<|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\cdot{\epsilon n\choose v}\cdot(v!)
.
Hence for every
G=S\cup f(S)\in\mathcal{G}
, we have (recall that we have set
\epsilon=\epsilon(\mathcal{B},\delta)>0
sufficiently small)

e(\mathcal{H}_{k}[G])\leq\epsilon|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\cdot{n\choose v}\cdot(v!)+O_{\mathcal{B},\delta}\left(n^{v-1/(m-1)}\right)\leq\eta n^{v}.

Recall that if
G
has more than
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, then we will have
e(\mathcal{H}_{k}[G])>\eta n^{v}
. Hence for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
G
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
.

(c)
\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert\leq n^{Cn^{k-1/(m-1)}}
for some
C=C(\mathcal{B},\delta)
.

Since
|\mathcal{G}|\leq|\mathcal{S}|\leq\left|\binom{V}{\leq C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|}\right|
with
|V|=2^{k}{n\choose k}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|}{|V|\choose s}\leq C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|\cdot{|V|\choose C_{0}\cdot n^{-1/(m-1)}\cdot|V|}

\displaystyle\leq n^{Cn^{k-1/(m-1)}}=n^{Cn^{k-1/(m-1)}}

for some
C=C(C_{0})=C(\mathcal{B},\delta)
.
∎

4.
Weak upper bound on the number of
k
-SAT functions using containers

In this section, we prove the upper bound in
Theorem

1.23
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For every fixed
k\geq 2
, the number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae on
n
variables is at most
2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
.

To prove this theorem, we first observe that every minimal formula is nearly simple.
To count simple minimal formulae, we apply the container method.
The container method requires us to characterize formulae with many instances of small non-minimal simple formulae, and these turn out to be related to the appearance of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
in an associated
k
-PDG.

4.1.
Minimal formulae are nearly simple

Lemma 4.2
.

The 2-blowup of a pair of clauses on the same set of
k
variables is non-minimal.

For example, the lemma tells us that the 2-blowup of
\{xyz,xy\overline{z}\}
is non-minimal.

Proof.

Up to relabeling and/or negating some of the variables, we can write the pair of clauses as
v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k}
and
\overline{v_{1}}\cdots\overline{v_{j}}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{k}
for some
1\leq j\leq k
.
Then its 2-blowup has clauses (among others)

v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k},v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k},v_{1}v_{2}^{\prime}v_{3}\cdots v_{k},\ldots,v_{1}\cdots v_{j-1}v_{j}^{\prime}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{k},\overline{v_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\overline{v_{j}^{\prime}}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{k}.

It is impossible to satisfy the first clause only, since it would involve setting
v_{1}=\cdots=v_{k}=1
and
v^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=v^{\prime}_{j}=0
, which would then satisfy the last clause.
So the 2-blowup is non-minimal.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In the above proof, if the initial pair of clauses differ by at least two negations, i.e.,
j\geq 2
, then the selected clauses of the 2-blowup form a simple non-minimal subformula.
We will use this fact later in
Section

4.4
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Every minimal
k
-SAT formula on
n
variables can be made simple by deleting
o(n^{k})
clauses.

Proof.

Let
G
be a minimal
k
-SAT formula on
n
variables.
By
Lemma

4.2
,
G
does not contain any 2-blowup of a pair of clauses on the same
k
variables.
It follows from
Lemma

3.2
that the number of
k
-subsets of variables that support at least two clauses in
G
is
o(n^{k})
. Removing all such clauses yields a simple subformula of
G
.
∎

4.2.
From
k
-SAT to
k
-PDGs

As hinted by
4.3
, for the container argument, we focus our attention on the following special type of formulae.

Definition 4.5

(Semisimple formula)
.

A
k
-SAT formula is
semisimple
if for every
k
-subset
S
of variables, one of the following is true:

(a)

There is no clause on
S
, or

(b)

There is exactly one clause on
S
, or

(c)

There are exactly two clauses on
S
and they differ by exactly one variable negation.

Example 4.6
.

The 3-SAT formula
\{x_{1}\overline{x_{2}}x_{3},x_{1}\overline{x}_{2}\overline{x}_{3},x_{1}x_{2}\overline{x_{4}}\}
is semisimple, whereas the formula
\{x_{1}\overline{x_{2}}x_{3},x_{1}x_{2}\overline{x_{3}}\}
is not semisimple.

We define a forgetful map from semisimple formulae to
k
-PDGs.

Definition 4.7
.

Define

\type\colon\{\text{semisimple $k$-SAT formula on $n$ variables}\}\to\{\text{$k$-PDGs on $n$ vertices}\}

as follows.
Given a semisimple formula
G
, let
\type(G)
be the
k
-PDG with vertices being the variables of
G
, where we add an undirected edge to every
k
-subset of variables supporting exactly one clause in
G
, and add a directed edge to every
k
-subset of variables support exactly two clauses in
G
, and for the latter, we direct the edge towards the variable where the two clauses differ by negation.

Example 4.8
.

The semisimple formula

abc,ab\overline{c},\overline{a}b\overline{d},bd\overline{e},\overline{b}d\overline{e}

has type

4.3.
Forbidden subgraphs and non-minimal formulae

The following proposition explains why
\mathcal{F}_{k}
plays a central role.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
G
be a
k
-SAT semisimple formula whose type lies in
\mathcal{F}_{k}
.
Then
G[2]
has a simple non-minimal subformula.

Example 4.10
.

We consider a
3\SAT
example.
An example of a semisimple formula with type
\vec{H}=(\{1,2,3,4\},\{12\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.5pt\hbox{$3$}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{17.44456pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt
