}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}\})
is the collection of
4
-clauses on
4
-variables
G=\{abc,ab\overline{c},\overline{b}cd,\overline{b}c\overline{d}\}
.
The
2
-blowup of
G
has the following simple non-minimal formula:
B=\{abc,ab^{\prime}c,ab\overline{c^{\prime}},\overline{b^{\prime}}cd,\overline{b^{\prime}}c^{\prime}\overline{d}\}
. We can observe that
B
is non-minimal by noting that to only satisfy
abc
, we must assign
a,b,c\mapsto 1
which forces us to assign
b^{\prime}\mapsto 0,c^{\prime}\mapsto 1
to avoid satisfying the second or third clause in
B
. However then we must assign
d\mapsto 0
to avoid satisfying the fourth clause in
B
and this combination of assignments satisfies the fifth clause in
B
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.9
.

Suppose the initialization edge in
\type(G)
is undirected. For clarity, we use two examples of
G
and
\type(G)
to illustrate this result that capture the fully generality of our argument. We then summarize the general algorithm for identifying a simple non-minimal formula in the
2
-blowup of a general
G
.

We first look at the case where the final edge in
\type(G)
does not introduce any new vertex. Let

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\{abcd,a\bar{b}ce,a\bar{b}c\bar{e},\bar{a}c\bar{d}f,\bar{a}c\bar{d}\bar{f},\bar{a}de\bar{f}\}

\displaystyle=\{C_{0},C_{1}^{(1)},C_{1}^{(2)},C_{2}^{(1)},C_{2}^{(2)},C_{3}\},

so that every label names the clause of the same order (i.e.,
C_{0}=abcd
, …,
C_{3}=\bar{a}de\bar{f}
). We have

Closure:
e_{3}=1456
.

We pick a simple non-minimal formula

G^{\prime}=\{\widetilde{C}_{0},\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)},\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2},\widetilde{C}_{3}\}\subset G[2]

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{0},\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)}

\displaystyle\in C_{0}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{1}

\displaystyle\in C_{1}^{(1)}[2]\cup C_{1}^{(2)}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{2}

\displaystyle\in C_{2}^{(1)}[2]\cup C_{2}^{(2)}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{3}

\displaystyle\in C_{3}[2],

using the following steps:

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{0}=abcd\in C_{0}[2]
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)}=a^{\prime}bcd,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(2)}=ab^{\prime}cd,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(3)}=abc^{\prime}d,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)}=abcd^{\prime}\in C_{0}[2]
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{3}=\overline{a^{\prime}}de\overline{f^{\prime}}\in C_{3}[2]
. We pick the variables
a^{\prime},d,e,f^{\prime}
because the clause
C_{4}=\overline{a}de\overline{f}\in G
uses positive literals
d,e
and negative literals
\overline{a},\overline{f}
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{1}=a\overline{b^{\prime}}c\overline{e}
. We pick the variables
a,b^{\prime},c
because
C_{1}^{(1)},C_{1}^{(2)}\in G
use positive literals
a,c
and negative literal
\overline{b}
. We pick the variable
e
because
\widetilde{C}_{3}
uses the variable
e
, and then pick the literal
\overline{e}
(instead of
e
) because
e
appears as a positive literal in
\widetilde{C}_{3}
, and we want the literal here to be the opposite of it.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{2}=\overline{a^{\prime}}c\overline{d^{\prime}}f^{\prime}
. We pick the variables
a^{\prime},c,d^{\prime}
because
C_{2}^{(1)},C_{2}^{(2)}\in G
use positive literal
c
and negative literals
\overline{a},\overline{d}
. We pick the variable
f^{\prime}
because
\widetilde{C}_{3}
uses the variable
f^{\prime}
, and then pick the literal
f^{\prime}
(instead of
\overline{f^{\prime}}
) because
\overline{f^{\prime}}
appears as a negative literal in
\widetilde{C}_{3}
, and we want the literal here to be the opposite of it.

We show that it is impossible to uniquely satisfy
\widetilde{C}_{0}\in G^{\prime}
. Suppose to the contrary that there exists a witness that maps
\widetilde{C}_{0}
to 1 and all other clauses in
G^{\prime}
to 0. We clearly have
a,b,c,d\mapsto 1
. Since all of
C_{0}^{(1)},\dots,C_{0}^{(4)}
are mapped to 0, we then have
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\mapsto 0
. Now since
\widetilde{C}_{1}
is mapped to 0, we have
e\mapsto 1
. Since
\widetilde{C}_{2}
is mapped to 0, we have
f^{\prime}\mapsto 0
. This forces
\widetilde{C}_{3}
to get mapped to 1, a contradiction.

We now look at the case where the final edge in
\type(G)
is pointed at some new vertex. Let

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\{abcd,\bar{a}b\bar{c}e,\bar{a}b\bar{c}\bar{e},ac\bar{d}f,ac\bar{d}\bar{f},b\bar{e}\bar{f}g,b\bar{e}\bar{f}\bar{g}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{C_{0},C_{1}^{(1)},C_{1}^{(2)},C_{2}^{(1)},C_{2}^{(2)},C_{3}^{(1)},C_{3}^{(2)}\right\},

so that every label names the clause of the same order. We have

Closure:
e_{3}=256\hbox{\hskip }{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}
.

We pick a simple non-minimal formula

G^{\prime}=\{\widetilde{C}_{0},\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)},\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\prime},\widetilde{C}_{2},\widetilde{C}_{3},\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\prime}\}\subset G[2]

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{0},\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)},\dots,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)}

\displaystyle\in C_{0}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in C_{1}^{(1)}[2]\cup C_{1}^{(2)}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{2}

\displaystyle\in C_{2}^{(1)}[2]\cup C_{2}^{(2)}[2],

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{3},\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in C_{3}^{(1)}[2]\cup C_{3}^{(2)}[2],

using the following steps.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{0}=abcd\in C_{0}[2]
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(1)}=a^{\prime}bcd,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(2)}=ab^{\prime}cd,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(3)}=abc^{\prime}d,\widetilde{C}_{0}^{(4)}=abcd^{\prime}\in C_{0}[2]
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{3}=b\bar{e^{\prime}}\bar{f^{\prime}}g
and
\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\prime}=b\bar{e}\bar{f^{\prime}}\bar{g}\in C_{3}[2]
. For
\widetilde{C}_{3}
, we pick the variables
b,e^{\prime},f^{\prime}
because the clauses
C_{3}^{(1)},C_{3}^{(2)}\in G
uses positive literal
b
and negative literals
\overline{e},\overline{f}
. We then pick the positive literal
g
(it does not matter whether we use
g
or
g^{\prime}
here). We obtain
\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\prime}
from
\widetilde{C}_{3}
by replacing the variable
e^{\prime}
with
e
and replacing the literal
g
with
\overline{g}
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{1}=\overline{a^{\prime}}b\overline{c^{\prime}}e^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\prime}=\overline{a^{\prime}}b\overline{c^{\prime}}e
. We pick
\widetilde{C}_{1}
exactly as how we would pick
\widetilde{C}_{1}
in the previous case. We obtain
\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\prime}
from
\widetilde{C}_{1}
by replacing the variable
e
with
e^{\prime}
.

•

Set
\widetilde{C}_{2}=ac\overline{d^{\prime}}f^{\prime}
. We pick
\widetilde{C}_{2}
exactly as how we would pick
\widetilde{C}_{2}
in the previous case.

We show that it is impossible to uniquely satisfy
\widetilde{C}_{0}\in G^{\prime}
. Suppose to the contrary that there exists a witness that maps
\widetilde{C}_{0}
to 1 and all other clauses in
G^{\prime}
to 0. By the same reasoning as before, we have
a,b,c,d\mapsto 1
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\mapsto 0
. Now since
\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{1}^{\prime}
are mapped to 0, we have
e,e^{\prime}\mapsto 0
. Since
\widetilde{C}_{2}
is mapped to 0, we have
f^{\prime}\mapsto 0
. This forces at least one of
\widetilde{C}_{3},\widetilde{C}_{3}^{\prime}
to get mapped to 1, a contradiction.

We can generalize this strategy to any such
G
. Let
C_{0}\in G
be the clause corresponding to the initialization edge
e_{0}
. Consider a witness
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that maps the original clause in
C_{0}[2]
to 1 and all other clauses in
G[2]
to 1. It follows that for every variable
v\in S
, we must assign one of
v,v^{\prime}
to 0 and the other to 1. Then for each of
e_{1},\dots,e_{j+1}
, we can pick one or two clauses appropriately from the 2-blowup of clauses in
G
corresponding to it, and show that it is impossible to map all of these clauses to 0. The strategy when the initialization edge in
\type(G)
is directed is very similar, and we do not elaborate here.
∎

If a semisimple formula
G
has type
\vec{H}
where
\vec{H}
contains many copies of some
\vec{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, then we’d like to show that
G
contains many non-minimal, simple formulae. We make this intuition quantitative below.

4.4.
Supersaturation

Now we show that, roughly speaking, if a formula has many clauses (with respect to some weighting), then it contains a small simple non-miminal subformula.

For a
k
-SAT formula
G
on
n
variables, define

\alpha_{i}(G){n\choose k}=\left|\left\{S\in{V\choose k}\mid G\text{ has exactly }i\text{ clauses with variables }S\right\}\right|.

Furthermore, define

\alpha_{2,1}(G){n\choose k}
to be the number of
S\in{V\choose k}
such that

G
has exactly two clauses with variables
S
and the pair differs on exactly on variable by negation.
Also set

\alpha_{2,2}(G)=\alpha_{2}(G)-\alpha_{2,1}(G).

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\theta>0
.
There exists
\delta=\delta(\epsilon,\theta)>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n
, if an
n
-variable
k
-SAT formula
G
satisfies
\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\alpha_{2,1}(G)\geq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)+\epsilon
,
then there is some simple non-minimal subformula
B
on at most
4k
vertices, such that
G
contains at least
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
as subformulae.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and
\theta>0
. Consider an
n
-variable
k
-SAT formula
G
such that
\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\alpha_{2,1}(G)\geq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)+\epsilon
. Up to deleting all the clauses of
G
not accounted for by
\alpha_{1}(G)
and
\alpha_{2,1}(G)
, we can assume that
G
is semisimple.

Let
\vec{H}=\type(G)
. Then
\vec{H}
has
\alpha_{1}(G)\binom{n}{k}
undirected edges and
\alpha_{2,1}(G)\binom{n}{k}
directed edges.
It follows by the definition of Turán density
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)
and a standard supersaturation argument that there is some
\vec{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
such that
\vec{H}
contains at least
\Omega(n^{v(\vec{F})})
copies of
\vec{F}
.
There are only finitely many isomorphism classes of semisimple formulae with type
\vec{F}
, so by pigeonhole, there is some
k
-SAT semisimple formula
G^{\prime}
, with
\type(G^{\prime})=\vec{F}
, such that
G
contains at least
\Omega(n^{v(\vec{F})})
copies of
G^{\prime}
as subformulae.
Then, by
Lemma

3.2
,
G
contains
\Omega(n^{2v(\vec{F})})
copies of
G^{\prime}[2]
as subformulae.
By
Proposition

4.9
, each copy of
G^{\prime}[2]
contains some simple non-minimal subformula.
Note that
G^{\prime}[2]
has at most
4k
variables since every element of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
has at most
2k
variables. The conclusion then follows immediately after another application of pigeonhole.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
There exists
\delta=\delta(\epsilon)>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n
, if an
n
-variable
k
-SAT formula has at least
\alpha_{i}(G)\geq\epsilon
for some
i\geq 3
or
\alpha_{2,2}(G)\geq\epsilon
, then there is some simple non-minimal subformula
B
on at most
2k
vertices such that
G
contains at least
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
as subformulae.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Consider an
n
-variable
k
-SAT formula
G
such that
\alpha_{i}(G)\geq\epsilon
for some
i\geq 3
, or
\alpha_{2,2}(G)\geq\epsilon
. By pigeonhole, there is some
2\leq j\leq k
such that
G
has at least
\Omega(\epsilon n^{k})
subformulae of the form
G^{\prime}=\{v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k}
and
\overline{v_{1}}\cdots\overline{v_{j}}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{k}\}
.
By
Lemma

3.2
,
G
contains
\Omega(n^{2k})
copies of
G^{\prime}[2]
as subformulae. Since every copy of
G^{\prime}[2]
has a non-minimal simple formula by
4.3
, the result follows.
∎

4.5.
Applying the container theorem

Recall the definition of the
\alpha_{i}
’s from the beginning of
Section

4.4
.

Definition 4.13
.

Define the
weight
of the formula of a
k
-SAT formula
G
by

\wt(G)=\alpha_{1}(G)+\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2}(G)+\log_{2}4\cdot\alpha_{3}(G)+\dots+\log_{2}(2^{k}+1)\cdot\alpha_{2^{k}}(G).

Given a
k
-SAT formula
G
on
n
variables, the number of simple
k
-SAT subformulae of
G
is

\prod_{j=1}^{2^{k}}(j+1)^{\alpha_{j}(G){n\choose k}}=2^{\wt(G){n\choose k}}

since there are
j+1
ways to choose at most one clause from
j
clauses. This motivates our definition of weight.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

4.1
that the number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae on
n
variables is at most
2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of all non-minimal simple formulae on at most
4k
variables, one for each isomorphism class (so that
\mathcal{B}
is finite).
Fix any
\epsilon>0
.
Pick
\delta>0
to be smaller than the minimum of the
\delta
’s chosen in
Lemma

4.11
(with
\theta=\log_{2}3
) and
Lemma

4.12
.
Applying the container result,
Theorem

3.4
, we find a collection
\mathcal{G}
of
n
-variable formulae on
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
such that

(a)

Every
\mathcal{B}
-free formula on
X
is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
, so in particular, every simple minimal formula on
X
is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
; and

(b)

For every
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
G
contains at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
, which implies, by
Lemmas

4.11
and
4.12
, that
\alpha_{1}(G)+\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)<\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+\epsilon
,
\alpha_{2,2}(G)<\epsilon
and
\alpha_{i}(G)<\epsilon
for all
i\geq 3
; and

(c)

\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert\leq 2^{o(n^{k})}
.

The number of simple minimal
k
-SAT subformula of each
G\in\mathcal{G}
is thus at most

2^{\wt(G)\binom{n}{k}}\leq 2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+O(\epsilon))\binom{n}{k}}.

By taking a union bound over all
G\in\mathcal{G}
, of which there are at most
2^{o(n^{k})}
, and noting that
\epsilon
can be taken to be arbitrarily small, we see that the number of simple minimal
k
-SAT formula on
n
variables is
2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
.

Finally, by
4.4
, we can obtain any minimal
k
-SAT formulae by adding
o(n^{k})
clauses to a simple minimal formula. This adds a neglible factor
\binom{2^{k}{n\choose k}}{\leq o(n^{k})}=2^{o(n^{k})}
. So the total number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae on
n
variables is
2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
.
∎

5.
A lower bound on the number of
k
-SAT functions

In this section, we prove the lower bound in
Theorem

1.23
, namely that for every fixed
k\geq 2
, there are at least
2^{(\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3)+o(1))\binom{n}{k}}
distinct
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables.

Definition 5.1
.

Given an
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
, let the
positive instance
of
\vec{H}
be the semisimple
k\SAT
formula
G\in\type^{-1}(\vec{H})
obtained as follows:

•

G
has the clause
v_{1}\cdots v_{k}
if and only if there exists an edge (directed or undirected) on
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
in
\vec{H}
;

•

G
has the clause
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{v}_{k}
if and only if
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-2.603pt\hbox{$v$}\hskip-2.603pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}}$}}\hskip-455.45732pt\hskip 455.45732pt}_{k}\in E(\vec{H})
.

We observe that the positive instance
G\in\type^{-1}(\vec{H})
on
n
vertices has
2^{\alpha(\vec{H}){n\choose k}}3^{\beta(\vec{H}){n\choose k}}=2^{(\alpha(\vec{H})+\log_{2}3\cdot\beta(\vec{H})){n\choose k}}
simple subformulae, as there are
\alpha(\vec{H}){n\choose k}

k
-subsets of variables in
G
that support exactly one clause, and
\beta(\vec{H}){n\choose k}

k
-subsets of variables in
G
that support exactly two clauses.

Lemma 5.2
.

Fix
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
on
n
vertices with positive instance
G\in\type^{-1}(\vec{H})
. Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the collection of simple subformulae of
G
. Then all
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
are minimal.

Proof.

Fix any
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
. To prove that
G^{\prime}
is minimal, we show that for every clause
C_{0}\in G^{\prime}
, we can find a witness
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{w}
maps
C_{0}
to 1 and all other clauses in
G^{\prime}
to
0
.

Fix any clause
C_{0}\in G^{\prime}
. First suppose that
C_{0}
is of the form
v_{1}\cdots v_{k}
. Let

X_{1}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\cup\{w:v_{i_{1}}\cdots v_{i_{k-1}}\overline{w}\in G\text{ for some }i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1}\in[k]\}.

Choose
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{w}(v)=1
if
v\in X_{1}
and
\mathsf{w}(v)=0
otherwise. We show that
\mathsf{w}
is a witness to
C_{0}
. For every
C\in G^{\prime}\setminus\{C_{0}\}
, there are three cases:

•

C
uses at least two variables outside
X_{1}
. In this case, since
C
has at most one negative literal, we know that
\mathsf{w}
maps
C
to 0.

•

C
uses exactly one variable
u_{0}
outside
X_{1}
. In this case, we show that the corresponding literal in
C
cannot be
\overline{u}_{0}
, so
\mathsf{w}
still maps
C
to 0. By contradiction, suppose that
C
contains
\overline{u}_{0}
, so
C
is of the form
v_{i_{1}}\cdots v_{i_{j}}w_{i_{j+1}}\cdots w_{i_{k-1}}\overline{u}_{0}
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{j}\in[k]
and some
w_{i_{j+1}},\dots,w_{i_{k-1}}\in X_{1}\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. However, if
j=k-1
, then
u_{0}\in X_{1}
, a contradiction; if
j<k-1
, then the underlying
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free, as it has a subgraph of the form

\qquad\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k},v_{i^{(j+1)}_{1}}\cdots v_{i^{(j+1)}_{k-1}}\hbox{\hskip 455.45732pt\hskip-3.71413pt\hbox{$w$}\hskip-3.71413pt\hskip-455.45732pt\raisebox{15.30566pt}{\hbox{$\scalebox{-1}{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raiseb 455.45732pt}_{0}\},

another contradiction.

•

C
only uses variables in
X_{1}
. In this case, to show that
\mathsf{w}
maps
C
to 0, it suffices to show that
C
cannot be monotone. By contradiction, suppose that
C
is of the form
v_{i_{1}}\dots v_{i_{j}}w_{i_{j+1}}\dots w_{i_{k}}
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{j}\in[k]
and some
w_{i_{j+1}},\dots,w_{i_{k}}\in X_{1}\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. However, if
j=k
, then
C=C_{0}
, a contradiction; if
j<k
, then the underlying
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free, as it has a subgraph of the form

\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k},v_{i^{(j+1)}_{1}}\cdots v_{i^{(j+1)}_{k-1}}\hbox{\hskip
