455.45732pt}_{i_{k}},v_{i_{1}}\dots v_{i_{j}}w_{i_{j+1}}\dots w_{i_{k}}\},

another contradiction.

We now suppose that
C_{0}
is of the form
v_{1}\dots v_{k-1}\overline{v}_{k}
. Let

X_{1}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}\}\cup\{w:v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G^{\prime}\}.

Choose
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{w}(v)=1
if
v\in X_{1}
and
\mathsf{w}(v)=0
otherwise. By an almost identical argument, we can show that
\mathsf{w}
is a witness to
C_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Fix
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
on
n
vertices with positive instance
G\in\type^{-1}(\vec{H})
. Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the collection of simple subformulae of
G
. If
G_{1}\neq G_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
, then
G_{1},G_{2}
encode distinct
k\SAT
functions.

Proof.

Consider two distinct formulae in
\mathcal{S}
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. First suppose that there exists a
k
-subset of variables
S\in{X\choose k}
such that
G_{1}
has a clause
C_{1}
on
S
but
G_{2}
does not. By the above claim,
C_{1}\cup G_{2}
is minimal, so there exists some
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that maps
C_{1}
to 1 (and thus
G_{1}
to 1) and
G_{2}
to 0. Hence
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
encode distinct
k
-SAT functions.

Now suppose that there exists a
k
-subset of variables
S\in{X\choose k}
such that
G_{2}
has a clause
C_{2}
on
S
but
G_{1}
does not. By the same argument, we know that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
encode distinct
k
-SAT functions.

Finally, suppose that the clauses in
G_{1}
and the clauses in
G_{2}
lie on exactly the same
k
-subsets of variables. In this case, since
G_{1}\neq G_{2}
, there must exist some
S=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\in{X\choose k}
such that
v_{1}\cdots v_{k}\in G_{1}
and
v_{1}\cdots\overline{v}_{k}\in G_{2}
(or vice versa). In this case, since
\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k}\}\cup G_{2}\setminus\{v_{1}\cdots\overline{v}_{k}\}
is minimal, there exists some
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
that maps
v_{1}\cdots v_{k}
to 1 (and thus
G_{1}
to 1) and
G_{2}\setminus\{v_{1}\cdots\overline{v}_{k}\}
to 0 (and thus
G_{2}
to 0). Hence
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
encode distinct
k
-SAT functions.
∎

Combining
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
implies that for
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free PDG
\vec{H}
with positive instance
G
, all simple subformulae of
G
are minimal and yield distinct
k\SAT
functions. This immediately implies the following lower bound.

Corollary 5.4
.

For every
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free
k
-PDG
\vec{H}
on
n
vertices, the number of distinct
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is at least
2^{(\alpha(\vec{H})+\log_{2}3\cdot\beta(\vec{H})){n\choose k}}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.23
.

The upper bound follows from
Theorem

4.1
.
To obtain the lower bound, notice that for every
n
, we have

\max\{\alpha(\vec{H})+\log_{2}3\cdot(\vec{H}):\vec{H}\text{ is an $\mathcal{F}_{k}$-free $k$-PDG on $n$ variables}\}\geq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3).

This is because we always have

\max\{\alpha(\vec{H})+\log_{2}3\cdot\beta(\vec{H}):\vec{H}\text{ is an $\mathcal{F}_{k}$-free $k$-PDG on $n$ variables}\}\\
\leq\max\{\alpha(\vec{H})+\log_{2}3\cdot\beta(\vec{H}):\vec{H}\text{ is an $\mathcal{F}_{k}$-free $k$-PDG on $n-1$ variables}\}

due to an averaging argument, so the left-hand side is non-increasing with respect to
n
.

Hence for all
n
, we can we can take an
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-free
k
-PDG
\vec{H}_{n}
with

\alpha(\vec{H}_{n})+\log_{2}3\cdot\beta(\vec{H}_{n})\geq\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3).

By
5.4
, the number of distinct
k
-SAT functions on
n
variables is at least
2^{\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\log_{2}3){n\choose k}}
.
∎

6.
Formulae that are far from unate

We prove
Theorem

1.24
in the following two sections. Let
\mathcal{I}(n)
be the set of minimal
k
-SAT formulae on
n
variables; notice that the number of
k\SAT
functions is at most
|\mathcal{I}(n)|
. In this section, we reduce the problem of bounding
|\mathcal{I}(n)|
to bounding the size of
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, the collection of minimal formulae that satisfy a given witness condition. This collection of formulae is defined as follows:

Definition 6.1
.

Fix a
k
-SAT formula
G
.
Given a variable
x
, let
m(x)
denote the number of clauses in
G
in which the positive literal
x
appears; analogously,
m(\overline{x})
is the number of clauses in
G
in which the negative literal
\overline{x}
appears.

Definition 6.2
.

For
\zeta>0
, let
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
be the collection of minimal
k
-SAT formulae where
m(x)\geq m(\overline{x})
for every variable
x
, and each clause in each formula has a witness
\mathsf{w}
(an element of
\{0,1\}^{n}
that satisfies only that clause in the formula) with fewer than
\zeta n
variables assigned to
1
.

We work to obtain a more precise estimate on the number of simple, minimal
k\SAT
formulae by analyzing the set of containers arising from applying
Theorem

3.4
, where
\mathcal{B}
is the set of simple non-minimal formulae on at most
2(k+1)
vertices.

In this section, we use these containers to enumerate those relatively “small” minimal formulae
G
, characterized by having
\alpha_{1}(G)
bounded away from
1
. We also bound the number of simple, minimal formulae that are “far from unate.” We are able to bound the remaining “large” and “almost unate” formulae in terms of
|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|
, and give an upper bound on this quantity in
Section

7
, which allows us to complete the proof of
Theorem

1.24
.

We make the notion of being “almost unate” precise below.

Definition 6.3
.

For every
\rho>0
, we say that a
k
-SAT formula
G
is
\rho
-
close to unate
it can be made unate by removing up to
\rho\binom{n}{k}
clauses.
Otherwise we say that
G
is
\rho
-
far from unate
.

In addition, for every variable
v
, we say that
v
is
\rho
-close to unate
if either
m(v)\leq\rho{n-1\choose k-1}
or
m(\overline{v})\leq\rho{n-1\choose k-1}
. Otherwise we say that
v
is
\rho
-far from unate
.

6.1.
Large, simple
\rho
-far from unate formulae have many subformulae in
\mathcal{B}

We prove that for some appropriately chosen
\rho>0
,
a>0
and
n
sufficiently large, every simple
\rho
-far from unate formula with more than
(1-a){n\choose k}
clauses has many non-unate
(k+1)
-cliques, where a
(k+1)
-clique is a simple formula with
k+1
clauses on
k+1
variables.

By
Lemma

3.2
, such a formula also contains many 2-blowups of non-unate
(k+1)
-cliques. Since the 2-blowup of every non-unate
(k+1)
-clique is non-minimal, such
G
has many simple non-minimal subformulae on
2(k+1)
vertices. The following definition of “link" formula will be used in our proof:

Definition 6.4
.

Given
k
-SAT formula
G
and variable
v
, the
positive link
of
v
, denoted
G_{v}
, is the
(k-1)\SAT
formula whose clauses are obtained by taking all clauses in
G
that contain literal
v
and removing
v
from each such clause. The
negative link
of
v
, denoted
G_{\overline{v}}
, is the
(k-1)\SAT
formula obtained by taking all all clauses in
G
that contain literal
\overline{v}
and removing
\overline{v}
from each such clause.

Proposition 6.5
.

For every
0<\rho<1
, we can take sufficiently small
a:=a(\rho)>0
such that the following holds for
n
sufficiently large. If
G
is a simple
\rho
-far from unate formula with at least
(1-a){n\choose k}
clauses, then for some
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\rho,a)>0
,
G
has at least
\delta_{1}{n\choose k+1}
non-unate
(k+1)
-cliques.

Proof.

Take
c=\rho/2k
,
N>c^{-1}
and
a>0
sufficiently small so that

1-kN\cdot a-c^{\frac{k}{k-1}}-(1-c)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}>0.

Fix some
G
that is
\rho
-far from unate with
|G|>(1-a){n\choose k}
. We first show that there are at least
cn
variables
v\in X
that are
c
-far from unate in
G
. Notice that we can obtain a unate subformula
G^{\prime}\subset G
by doing the following: for every variable
v\in X
, if
m(v)\geq m(\overline{v})
, then we delete all clauses in
G
containing the literal
\overline{v}
; otherwise we delete all clauses in
G
containing the literal
v
. If there are less than
cn
variables
v\in X
that are
c
-far from unate in
G
, then we can delete at most

\displaystyle n\cdot c{n-1\choose k-1}+cn\cdot{n-1\choose k-1}\leq\rho{n\choose k}

clauses to make
G
unate, which contradicts the fact that
G
is
\rho
-far from unate.
Also notice that there are at most
n/N
vertices
v\in X
with
m(v)+m(\overline{v})\leq(1-Na){n-1\choose k-1}
, as otherwise we would have

|G|<\frac{1}{k}\left(\frac{n}{N}(1-Na){n-1\choose k-1}+\left(1-\frac{1}{N}\right)n{n-1\choose k-1}\right)\leq(1-a){n\choose k}.

Combining the above tells us that there are at least
\left(c-N^{-1}\right)n
variables that are
c
-far from unate with
m(v)+m(\overline{v})>(1-Na){n-1\choose k-1}
.

For every such
v
, consider the positive and negative link
(k-1)
-graphs
G_{v}
and
G_{\overline{v}}
. By the Kruskal–Katona theorem in the form of
Theorem

3.3
, the number of
k
-cliques in
G_{v}
is at most
\left((k-1)!|G_{v}|/(n-1)^{k-1}\right)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}\cdot(n-1)^{k}/k!
and the number of
k
-cliques in
G_{\overline{v}}
is at most
\left((k-1)!|G_{\overline{v}}|/(n-1)^{k-1}\right)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}\cdot(n-1)^{k}/k!
. Since
|G_{v}|=m(v)
,
|G_{\overline{v}}|=m(\overline{v})
and
v
is
c
-far from unate, we know that
|G_{v}|,|G_{\overline{v}}|\geq c{n-1\choose k-1}
. By convexity, the number of monochromatic
k
-cliques in
G_{v}\cup G_{\overline{v}}
is at most

\left(c^{\frac{k}{k-1}}+(1-c)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}+o(1)\right){n-1\choose k}.

Meanwhile, since
|G_{v}|+|G_{\overline{v}}|=m(v)+m(\overline{v})>(1-Na){n-1\choose k-1}
, the number of
k
-cliques in
G_{v}\cup G_{\overline{v}}
is at least
(1-Nk\cdot a){n-1\choose k}
.
The number of non-monochromatic
k
-cliques in
G_{v}\cup G_{\overline{v}}
is thus at least

\left(1-Nk\cdot a-c^{\frac{k}{k-1}}-(1-c)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}+o(1)\right){n-1\choose k}.

Hence
G
has at least

\frac{1}{k+1}\left(c-N^{-1}\right)n\cdot\left(1-Nk\cdot a-c^{\frac{k}{k-1}}-(1-c)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}+o(1)\right){n-1\choose k}

non-unate
(k+1)
-cliques. Choosing any
0<\delta_{1}<\left(c-N^{-1}\right)\left(1-Nk\cdot a-c^{\frac{k}{k-1}}-(1-c)^{\frac{k}{k-1}}\right)
yields the result.
∎

It remains to show that the 2-blowup of any non-unate simplex is non-minimal.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
H
be a non-unate simple formula with
k+1
clauses on
k+1
variables. Then the 2-blowup
H[2]
is non-minimal.

Proof.

Let
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k+1}\}
be the variable set of
H
, and
\{v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},\dots,v_{k+1},v_{k+1}^{\prime}\}
be the variable set of
H[2]
. Up to negating and/or relabeling some of the variables,
H
contains a subformula
H^{\prime}
comprising
3
clauses: a clause
C_{1}=v_{2}\cdots v_{k-1}v_{k}v_{k+1}
, a clause
C_{2}
with positive literal
v_{1}
and variables
v_{2},v_{3},\ldots,v_{k-1},v_{k}
(that may appear as positive or negative literals), and a clause
C_{3}
with negative literal
\overline{v_{1}}
and variables
v_{2},v_{3},\ldots,v_{k-2},v_{k-1},v_{k+1}
(that may appear as positive or negative literals).

We show that
H^{\prime}[2]
is non-minimal. Since

\{v_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k+1},v_{2}^{\prime}v_{3}\cdots v_{k+1},v_{2}v_{3}^{\prime}\cdots v_{k+1},\dots,v_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k+1}^{\prime}\}\subset H^{\prime}[2],

if
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{k+1}
is a witness to the clause
v_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k+1}\in H^{\prime}[2]
, then we must have

\mathsf{w}(v_{2})=\dots=\mathsf{w}(v_{k+1})=1,

\mathsf{w}(v_{2}^{\prime})=\dots=\mathsf{w}(v_{k+1}^{\prime})=0.

Now if
\mathsf{w}(v_{1})=1
, then there must be a clause in the 2-blowup of
C_{2}
that gets mapped to 1. If
\mathsf{w}(v_{1})=0
, then there must be a clause in the 2-blowup of
C_{3}
that gets mapped to 1. This is a contradiction, so there is no witness
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{k+1}
to the clause
v_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k+1}\in H^{\prime}[2]
.
∎

Combining
Lemma

3.2
,
6.5
and
6.6
, we have the following:

Corollary 6.7
.

For every
\rho>0
, we can take sufficiently small
a:=a(\rho)>0
such that the following holds for
n
sufficiently large. If
G
is a simple
\rho
-far from unate formula with at least
(1-a){n\choose k}
clauses, then there exists
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(\rho,a)
such that
G
has at least
\delta_{2}n^{2(k+1)}
simple non-minimal subformulae on
2(k+1)
variables.

6.2.
Reducing the problem to bounding the size of some
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)

We are now able to give an upper bound on the number of minimal
k
-SAT formulae that do not satisfy a particular witness condition, thereby reducing the problem of bounding the size of
\mathcal{I}(n)
to bounding the size of
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
.

We first show that for any collection of
\rho
-close to unate formulae
\mathcal{G}
, all but few elements of
\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}
lie in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
up to replacing some of the variables by their negations.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any collection
\mathcal{G}
of
\rho
-close to unate formulae and
\zeta>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\{H\subset G:H\textup{ is minimal}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{G}|\cdot\left({n\choose\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}\left({\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}+1\right)2^{{n\choose k}-{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}}2^{\rho{n\choose k}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|.

Proof.

For every
G\in\mathcal{G}
, there exists a negation of some variables that maps
G
to some formula
G^{\prime}
having at most
\rho{n\choose k}
non-monotone clauses. Without loss of generality, we assume that
G^{\prime}
has the greatest number of non-monotone clauses. In other words, no negation of variables maps
G
to a formula having more non-monotone clauses. Notice that this automatically implies that
m(x)\geq m(\overline{x})
in
G^{\prime}
for every
x\in X
.

We give an upper bound on the number of minimal subformulae of
G^{\prime}
that do not lie in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
. By
6.2
, for all
H\notin\mathcal{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, there exists a clause in
H
and a witness
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to that clause, such that
\mathsf{w}
assigns at least
\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor
variables to 1. Let
(v_{1},\dots,v_{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor})
be the lexicographically smallest
(\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor)
-tuple of variables, such that there exists some witness
\mathsf{w}
to some clause in
H
with
\mathsf{w}(v_{1})=\dots=\mathsf{w}(v_{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor})=1
. Notice that since
\mathsf{w}
is a witness to some clause in
H
, it cannot map two clauses in
H
to 1. Hence at most one clause of the form
v_{i_{1}}\cdots v_{i_{k}}
can appear in
H
.

We obtain a minimal formula
H\subset G^{\prime}
with
H\notin\mathcal{\mathcal{I}}^{*}(n,\zeta)
by making the following set of choices.

•

We pick the non-monotone clauses in
H
. There are at most
2^{\rho{n\choose k}}
such ways to make this selection since
G^{\prime}
has at most
\rho{n\choose k}
non-monotone clauses.

•

We pick the lexicographically smallest subset of literals
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}\}
so that there exists some witness
\mathsf{w}
to a clause in
H
with
\mathsf{w}(v_{1})=\dots=\mathsf{w}(v_{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor})=1
. There are at most
{n\choose\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}
ways to make such a choice.

•

We choose the monotone clauses of the form
v_{i_{1}}\cdots v_{i_{k}}
, of which there are
({\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}+1)
possible subsets since we can include at most one such clause.

•

Finally, we choose the remaining monotone clauses, with variables not entirely drawn from
v_{1},\ldots,v_{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}
.

This yields the following upper bound on the number of minimal formulae
H\subset G^{\prime}
with
H\not\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
:

{n\choose\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}\left({\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}+1\right)2^{{n\choose k}-{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}}2^{\rho{n\choose k}}.

Hence all but at most
|\mathcal{G}|\cdot\left({n\choose\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}\left({\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}+1\right)2^{{n\choose k}-{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}}2^{\rho{n\choose k}}\right)
elements of
\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}
lie in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
up to replacing some of the variables by their negations, and we have the desired bound.
∎

We now state our main reduction step.

Proposition 6.9
.

For every
\zeta>0
and
\theta>\log_{2}3
with
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n
we have

|\mathcal{I}(n)|\leq 2^{(1-\epsilon)\binom{n}{k}}+2^{n}\left\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\right\rvert.

Proof.

Given
\zeta>0
and
\theta>\log_{2}3
with
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
, we first choose
\rho>0
sufficiently smaller than
\zeta
and
\theta
, and then choose
\epsilon>0
sufficiently smaller than
\rho
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of all simple non-minimal
k\SAT
formulae on at most
2(k+1)
vertices. Applying
Theorem

3.4
with
\mathcal{B}
and some
\delta>0
sufficiently smaller than
\epsilon
, we obtain a collection
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)
of formulae that meets the following conditions:

•

Every
\mathcal{B}
-free formula with
n
variables is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}
;

•

For every
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
G
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}
copies of
B
;

•

\left\lvert\mathcal{G}\right\rvert\leq n^{C(\mathcal{B},\delta)n^{k-1/(m(\mathcal{B})-1)}}
.

Moreover, due to
Lemma

4.11
,
Lemma

4.12
and the assumption that
\delta
is sufficiently smaller than
\epsilon
, every
G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)
also meets the following conditions:

•

\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)<1+\epsilon
;

•

\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2,2}(G)+\log_{2}4\cdot\alpha_{3}(G)+\dots+\log_{2}(2^{k}+1)\cdot\alpha_{2^{k}}(G)<\epsilon
.

Every formula in
\mathcal{I}(n)
is
\mathcal{B}
-free, so it is a subformula of some
G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)
. Hence we have

|\mathcal{I}(n)|\leq\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}\right|.

Define a partition
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)=\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}\sqcup\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}

\displaystyle=\left\{G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta):\alpha_{1}(G)\leq 1-2\epsilon-\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}

\displaystyle=\left\{G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta):\alpha_{1}(G)>1-2\epsilon-\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}\right\}.

We first give an upper bound on the number of minimal subformulae of every
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}
. According to
4.4
, every minimal
k\SAT
formula can be made simple by deleting
o(n^{k})
clauses. This implies that for every
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle(\text{\# of minimal subformulae of $G$})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{t=1}^{o(n^{k})}{2^{k}{n\choose k}\choose t}\right)\cdot(\text{\# of simple minimal subformulae of $G$})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}{n\choose k}}\cdot(\text{\# of simple minimal subformulae of $G$})

for sufficiently large
n
.

We now show that every
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}
has
\wt(G)<1-\epsilon
, so
G
has at most
2^{(1-\epsilon){n\choose k}}
simple minimal subformulae. For all
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}
, since

\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2,2}(G)+\log_{2}4\cdot\alpha_{3}(G)+\dots+\log_{2}(2^{k}+1)\cdot\alpha_{2^{k}}(G)<\epsilon,

we have

\wt(G)<\alpha_{1}(G)+\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)+\epsilon.

If
\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)\leq 1-2\epsilon
, then since
\theta>\log_{2}3
, we clearly have
\wt(G)<1-\epsilon
. If
\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)>1-2\epsilon
, then since
\alpha_{1}(G)\leq 1-2\epsilon-\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}
, we have
\alpha_{2,1}(G)>\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}
, so that

\wt(G)<\alpha_{1}(G)+\theta\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)-(\theta-\log_{2}3)\cdot\alpha_{2,1}(G)+\epsilon<(1+\epsilon)-3\epsilon+\epsilon=1-\epsilon.

Hence every
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}
has
\wt(G)<1-\epsilon
, which implies that
G
has at most
2^{(1-\epsilon){n\choose k}}
simple minimal subformulae. Therefore, when
n
is sufficiently large, every
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}
has at most
2^{\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right){n\choose k}}
minimal subformulae, and we have the upper bound

(6.1)

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}\right|\leq|\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}|\cdot 2^{\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right){n\choose k}}.

We now give an upper bound on
\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}\right|
. For all
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}
, we know that

\alpha_{1}(G)>1-2\epsilon-\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}.

Choose a simple subformula
G_{s}\subset G
with
|G_{s}|=\alpha_{1}(G){n\choose k}
.
Since
G
has at most
\delta n^{v(B)}\leq\delta n^{2(k+1)}
copies of any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we know that
G_{s}\subset G
has a total of at most

|\mathcal{B}|\cdot\delta n^{2(k+1)}\overset{(*)}{<}\delta_{2}\left(\frac{\rho}{2},2\epsilon+\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}\right)n^{2(k+1)}

simple non-minimal formulae on
2(k+1)
variables, where
\delta_{2}
is defined in
6.7
. Here (*) is due to the fact that
\delta
is much smaller than
\epsilon
(and thus also
\rho,\theta
). Since
\epsilon
is significantly smaller than
\rho
,
6.7
applies, so
G_{s}
is
\frac{\rho}{2}
-close to unate.

Meanwhile, since
\alpha_{1}(G)
is large,
\alpha_{2,1}(G),\alpha_{2,2}(G),\alpha_{3}(G),\dots,\alpha_{2^{k}}(G)
are all very small. In particular, we have

\alpha_{2,1}(G)<\frac{1}{\theta}\left((1+\epsilon)-\left(1-2\epsilon-\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta-\log_{2}3}\right)\right)=\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta}+\frac{3\epsilon}{\theta(\theta-\log_{2}3)}

and

\log_{2}3\cdot\alpha_{2,2}(G)+\log_{2}4\cdot\alpha_{3}(G)+\dots+\log_{2}(2^{k}+1)\cdot\alpha_{2^{k}}(G)<\epsilon.

This gives

\displaystyle|G\setminus G_{s}|

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha_{2,1}(G)+\alpha_{2,2}(G)){n\choose k}+2\alpha_{3}(G){n\choose k}+\dots+(2^{k}-1)\alpha_{2^{k}}(G){n\choose k}

\displaystyle\overset{(**)}{<}\frac{\rho}{2}{n\choose k},

where (**) is due to the fact that
\epsilon
is sufficiently smaller than
\rho
.

Combining the above, we know that every
G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}
is
\rho
-close to unate. Hence
\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}
is a family of
\rho
-close to unate formulae. By
Lemma

6.8
, we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}\right|&\leq|\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}|\cdot\left({n\choose\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor}\left({\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}+1\right)2^{{n\choose k}-{\left\lfloor\zeta n\right\rfloor\choose k}}2^{\rho{n\choose k}}\right)\\
&\qquad+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|\\
&\overset{(***)}{\leq}|\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}|\cdot 2^{(1-\epsilon){n\choose k}}+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|,\end{split}

for sufficiently large
n
, where (***) is due to the fact that
\epsilon
is sufficiently smaller than
\rho
.

Since

|\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}|,|\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}|\leq|\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)|\leq n^{C(\mathcal{B},\delta)n^{k-1/(m(\mathcal{B})-1)}},

combining
Equation

6.1
and
Equation

6.2
, we have the following upper bound on
|\mathcal{I}(n)|
for
n
sufficiently large:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\bigcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{B},\delta)}\{H\subset G:H\text{ is minimal}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{G}^{\text{sm}}|\cdot 2^{\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right){n\choose k}}+|\mathcal{G}^{\text{lg}}|\cdot 2^{(1-\epsilon){n\choose k}}+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq n^{C(\mathcal{B},\delta)n^{k-1/(m(\mathcal{B})-1)}}\left(2^{\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right){n\choose k}}+2^{(1-\epsilon){n\choose k}+n}\right)+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{3}\right){n\choose k}}+2^{n}|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|.\qed

7.
Nearly unate formulae

In this section we prove the following recursive bound.

Theorem 7.1
.

There exist
\zeta>0
and
c>0
such that for all
n
, we have

|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|\leq 2^{n\choose k}+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|\\
+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c)k{n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|+\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-cn^{k-2}\right].

Our approach in this section extends the arguments in Ilinca–Kahn
[
18
, Section 8]
.
To obtain the above bound, we consider a sequence of subcollections of
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
and bound the size of each one, showing that most of the formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
are monotone.

(1)

We first show that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
use each of the
n
variables a comparable number of times amongst their clauses, thus disregarding those formulae that have very few occurrences of at least one variable.

(2)

We then observe that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
have very few non-monotone clauses that contain at most
k-2
negative literals.

(3)

Next, we show that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
have very few non-monotone clauses that contain
k-1
or
k
negative literals.

(4)

Finally, we give an upper bound on the number of non-monotone formulae that satisfy the above three conditions.

(5)

We combine the above to obtain a recursive bound on
|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|
that gives the desired stability result.

We let
H(\cdot)
denote the binary entropy function,
H(p)=-p\log_{2}p-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)
. Recall the standard inequality

\sum_{t\leq pn}{n\choose i}\leq 2^{H(p)n}\quad\text{ for all }0<p\leq 1/2\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}.

We also recall several definitions regarding
k\SAT
formulae. Let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
denote the set of
n
variables on which we count the
k
-SAT formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
. For every clause
C
in a minimal formula
G
, we say that
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is a witness to
G
if
\mathsf{w}
maps
C
to 1 and all other clauses in
G
to
0
.

The following steps
Section

7.1
to
Section

7.4
roughly correspond to Steps 1 to 5 in
[
18
, Section 8]
.

7.1.
Most minimal formulae have large support for each variable

We start by observing that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
have lots of clauses involving
v
for every variable
v\in X
. More precisely, we show that for most formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, each variable
v\in X
is used in at least
\frac{1}{10k}{n-1\choose k-1}
clauses.

Definition 7.2
.

Let

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)=\left\{G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta):\text{each variable $v\in X$ is used at least $\frac{1}{10k}{n-1\choose k-1}$ times in $G$}\right\}.

Lemma 7.3
.

We have

|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)|<\exp_{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}{n-1\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|.

Proof.

We count the number of
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
. There are at most

n\sum_{t\leq\frac{1}{10k}{n-1\choose k-1}}{{2^{k}{n-1\choose k-1}}\choose t}<\exp_{2}\left[H\left(\frac{1}{10k\cdot 2^{k}}\right)2^{k}{n-1\choose k-1}\right]<\exp_{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}{n-1\choose k-1}\right]

ways to choose a variable
v\in X
that occurs fewer than
\frac{1}{10k}{n-1\choose k-1}
times in
G
, along with the clauses containing
v
. The remaining clauses in
G
form a minimal formula on
n-1
variables, so there are at most
|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|
ways to choose the remaining clauses. Combining gives the desired bound.
∎

Using the fact that every variable appears in many clauses in
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
we are able to conclude that for formulae in
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)
, most clauses are monotone. We make the following more precise observations that will prove useful in subsequent steps.

Lemma 7.4
.

For every
G\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, the following are true.

(a)

For every
k-1
variables
v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}
, there are at most
\zeta n
variables
w
such that
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
. The same bound holds for
w
’s with
\overline{v}_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
,
w
’s with
v_{1}\overline{v}_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
,
w
’s with
\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
, and so on.

(b)

For every variable
v
and
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, there are at most
\zeta n^{k-1}
clauses in
G
containing the positive literal
v
and exactly
j
negative literals.

(c)

For every variable
v
and
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, there are at most
\zeta n^{k-1}
clauses in
G
containing the negative literal
\overline{v}
and exactly
j
negative literals other than
\overline{v}
.

(d)

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, there are at most
\zeta n^{k}
clauses in
G
having exactly
i
negative literals.

(e)

G
has at most
k\zeta n^{k}
non-monotone clauses.

(f)

For every variable
v\in X
,
G
has at least
\frac{1}{20k}{n-1\choose k-1}-(k-1)\zeta n^{k-1}
monotone clauses containing
v
.

Proof.

We prove these properties of formulae
G\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
.

(a)

If there are more than
\zeta n
such
w
, then consider a witness
\mathsf{w}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to some
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}_{0}
. For any other
w\neq w_{0}
such that
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
, we must have
\mathsf{w}(w)=1
, a contradiction. Other cases are similar.

(b)

For any
k-2
literals
\overline{v}_{1},\dots,\overline{v}_{j-1},v_{j},\ldots,v_{k-2}
, by (a),
G
contains at most
\zeta n
clauses of the form
v\overline{v}_{1}\cdots\overline{v}_{j-1}v_{j}\cdots v_{k-2}\overline{w}
. There are at most
n^{k-2}
ways to choose these
k-2
literals.

(c)

Same as above.

(d)

This easily follows from (b) and (c).

(e)

Follows from (d).

(f)

Since
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, we have
m(v)\geq m(\overline{v})
for all variables
v\in X
. Since
G\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, we have
m(v)+m(\overline{v})\geq\frac{1}{10k}{n-1\choose k-1}
. This implies
m(v)\geq\frac{1}{20k}{n-1\choose k-1}
. By (b), there are at most
(k-1)\zeta n^{k-1}
non-monotone clauses that contain the positive literal
v
.

∎

7.2.
Most minimal formulae have few clauses with negated literals

We will eventually show that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
have very few clauses containing any negative literals. In this subsection, we give an upper bound on the number of formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
having a large numbers of clauses containing
i
negative literals, for any
1\leq i\leq k-2
.

Definition 7.5
.

For every
\beta_{1}>0
, we define

\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,1}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})=\left\{G\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(\zeta):\text{for all $u\in X$, $G$ contains at most $\beta_{1}n^{k-1}$ clauses}\right.\\
\left.\text{of the form }\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\right\}.

For every
i\in\{2,\dots,k-2\}
and
\beta_{i}>0
, we define

\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,i}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})=\left\{G\in\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1}):\text{for all $\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\}\in{X\choose i}$, }\right.\\
\left.\text{$G$ contains at most $\beta_{i}n^{k-i}$ clauses of the form }\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}\right\}.

For every vector
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k-2})
, we define
\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})=\bigcap_{i=2}^{k-2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,i}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})
.

We will first show that most formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)
are in
\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
for every
\beta_{1}>0
and sufficiently small
\zeta
.

7.2.1.
Formulae with lots of clauses
\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}

Lemma 7.6
.

For every
\beta_{1}>0
, there exist
\zeta>0
and
c>0
such that

|\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})|\leq\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|.

Proof.

For every
\beta_{1}>0
, we choose
\xi>0
sufficiently smaller than
\beta_{1}
, choose
\theta_{1}>0
sufficiently smaller than
\xi
, and then choose
\zeta>0
sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{1}
. Take a small constant
c=c(\beta_{1},\xi,\theta_{1},\zeta)>0
.

For every
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
, by definition, there exists some
u\in X
such that
G
contains more than
\beta_{1}n^{k-1}
clauses of the form
\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}
. Set
X_{u}=X\setminus\{u\}
and

\displaystyle N_{u}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}\}\subset X_{u}:uv_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\in G\right\},

\displaystyle N_{u}^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}\}\subset X_{u}:\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\in G\right\},

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2}\}\subset X_{u}:\text{codeg}_{N_{u}^{+}}(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2}\})<\theta_{1}n\right\},

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2}\}\subset X_{u}:\text{codeg}_{N_{u}^{-}}(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2}\})<\theta_{1}n\right\},

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle={X_{u}\choose k-2}\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2}),

where
\text{codeg}_{N_{u}^{+}}
(resp.
\text{codeg}_{N_{u}^{-}}
) is defined to be

\text{codeg}_{N_{u}^{+}}(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2}\})=|\{w\in X_{u}:\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2},w\}\in N_{u}^{+}\}|.

Since there are at most

|S_{2}|\theta_{1}n+\left({n-1\choose k-2}-|S_{2}|\right)n\leq(|S_{1}|+|T|)n+\frac{\theta_{1}n^{k-1}}{(k-2)!}

clauses of the form
\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}
, we have

\beta_{1}n^{k-1}\leq(|S_{1}|+|T|)n+\frac{\theta_{1}n^{k-1}}{(k-2)!},

and at least one of the following:

(1)

|T|>\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
;

(2)

|T|\leq\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
and
|S_{1}|\geq\left(\beta_{1}-\frac{(k-2)!+1}{(k-2)!}\cdot\theta_{1}\right)n^{k-2}>\xi n^{k-2}
. (Here we used the fact that
\theta_{1}
is sufficiently smaller than
\xi
.)

Claim 7.7
.

For sufficiently small
c>0
, there are at most
\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]
formulae
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
where
|T|>\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
, for
T
defined as above.

Proof.

Consider one such
G
.
Notice that if
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2},v_{k-1}\}\in N_{u}^{+}
and
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-2},v_{k}\}\in N_{u}^{-}
, then we cannot have
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-2}v_{k-1}v_{k}\in G
due to the non-minimal formula

\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k-2}v_{k-1}u,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-2}v_{k}\overline{u},v_{1}\cdots v_{k-2}v_{k-1}v_{k}\}

(as it is impossible to satisfy only the first clause). Hence after specifying (1)
u
and
T
, (2) the elements in
N_{u}^{+}
containing some element in
T
, and (3) the elements in
N_{u}^{-}
containing some element in
T
, we know at least

\frac{1}{{k\choose 2}}\cdot|T|\cdot\theta_{1}n\cdot\theta_{1}n>\frac{1}{{k\choose 2}}\cdot\theta_{1}n^{k-2}\cdot\theta_{1}n\cdot\theta_{1}n=\frac{\theta_{1}^{3}n^{k}}{{k\choose 2}}

monotone clauses that cannot belong to
G
.
To specify some
G\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)\backslash\mathcal{I}_{2,1}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
where
|T|>\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
, it suffices to make the following choices.

•

We choose a variable
u\in X
where
G
has more than
\beta_{1}n^{k-1}
clauses
\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}
. There are at most
n
such choices.

•

We pick the set
T
. Since each element of
T
is a set of
k-2
variables, there are at most
2^{{n\choose k-2}}
choices for
T
.

•

We choose the elements of
N_{u}^{+}
and
N_{u}^{-}
that contain some element of
T
. Since
N_{u}^{+},N_{u}^{-}\subset{X_{u}\choose k-1}
and
T\subset{X_{u}\choose k-2}
, the number of choices is at most
2^{|T|n}\cdot 2^{|T|n}\leq\exp_{2}[2\theta_{1}n^{k-1}]
.

•

We choose the non-monotone clauses in
G
.
Lemma

7.4
tells us that
G
has at most
k\zeta n^{k}
non-monotone clauses, so the number of such choices is at most
\binom{(2^{k}-1)\binom{n}{k}}{\leq k\zeta n^{k}}=\exp_{2}[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta))\binom{n}{k}]
.

•

Finally, we choose the monotone clauses in
G
. Per above, we know that there are at least
\theta_{1}^{3}n^{k}/{k\choose 2}
monotone clauses that cannot belong to
G
; this implies the simple upper bound

\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-\frac{\theta_{1}^{3}n^{k}}{{k\choose 2}}\right]
.

Hence the number of possible
G
is at most

n\cdot 2^{{n\choose k-2}}\cdot\exp_{2}[2\theta_{1}n^{k-1}]\cdot\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta))\binom{n}{k}+{n\choose k}-\frac{\theta_{1}^{3}n^{k}}{{k\choose 2}}\right]\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right],

where (*) is due to the fact that
\zeta
is sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{1}
, and
c
is small.
∎

Claim 7.8
.

For sufficiently small
c>0
, there are at most
\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|
formulae
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
where
|T|\leq\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
and
|S_{1}|>\xi n^{k-2}
, for
S_{1},T
defined as above.

Proof.

Consider one such
G
. We can uniquely specify
G
via the following 5 choices:

(1)

u
;

(2)

the monotone clauses containing
u
;

(3)

the clauses containing
\overline{u}
where all other literals are positive;

(4)

the clauses containing (i) either
u
or
\overline{u}
; (ii) some negative literal other than
\overline{u}
;

(5)

the clauses not involving the variable
u
.

Observe that choosing (2) and (3) is equivalent to choosing
N_{u}^{+}
and
N_{u}^{-}
. Since the number of monotone clauses in
G
using
u
is at least
\frac{1}{20}{n-1\choose k-1}-(k-1)\zeta n^{k-1}
according to
Lemma

7.4
, and also at most
\frac{1}{k-1}(|S_{1}|\theta_{1}n+({n-1\choose k-2}-|S_{1}|)n)
, we have the inequality

\frac{1}{20}{n-1\choose k-1}-(k-1)\zeta n^{k-1}\leq\frac{1}{k-1}\left(|S_{1}|\theta_{1}n+\left({n-1\choose k-2}-|S_{1}|\right)n\right).

After some rearrangement, this gives an upper bound on the size of
S_{1}
and thus a lower bound on the size of
S_{2}
:

\displaystyle|S_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{1-\theta_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{(k-2)!}+(k-1)^{2}\zeta-\frac{0.9}{20(k-2)!}\right)n^{k-2},

\displaystyle|S_{2}|\overset{(*)}{\geq}{n\choose k-2}-\theta_{1}n^{k-2}-\frac{1}{1-\theta_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{(k-2)!}+(k-1)^{2}\zeta-\frac{0.9}{20(k-2)!}\right)n^{k-2}\overset{(**)}{\geq}\xi n^{k-2}.

Here (*) is due to the fact that
|T|\leq\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
, and (**) is due to the fact that
\theta_{1}
is sufficiently smaller than
\xi
, and
\zeta
is sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{1}
. We then count the ways to choose such
G
.

•

We choose a variable
u\in X
where
G
has more than
\beta_{1}n^{k-1}
clauses
\overline{u}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}
. There are at most
n
such choices.

•

We choose sets
S_{1},S_{2},T
. There are at most
2^{{n\choose k-2}}\cdot 2^{{n\choose k-2}}=4^{{n\choose k-2}}
to choose
S_{1},S_{2}
(and consequently
T
).

•

We then choose which elements of
N_{u}^{+}
meet
S_{1}\cup T
and which elements of
N_{u}^{-}
meet
S_{2}\cup T
. The number of such choices is at most

\displaystyle 2^{|T|n}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\theta_{1}n}{n\choose t}\right)^{|S_{1}|}\cdot 2^{|T|n}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\theta_{1}n}{n\choose t}\right)^{|S_{2}|}

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[2\theta_{1}n^{k-1}+H(\theta_{1})n(|S_{1}|+|S_{2}|)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[2\theta_{1}n^{k-1}+H(\theta_{1})n^{k-1}\right].

•

We then choose the elements of
N_{u}^{-}
that only meet
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
and the elements of
N_{u}^{+}
that only meet
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
. By the Kruskal–Katona theorem in the form of
Theorem

3.3
, the number of such choices is at most

\displaystyle\exp_{2}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{((k-2)!|S_{1}\setminus S_{2}|)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}}{(k-1)!}+\frac{((k-2)!|S_{2}\setminus S_{1}|)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}}{(k-1)!}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[\frac{((k-2)!)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}}{(k-1)!}\left(|S_{1}\setminus S_{2}|^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+|S_{2}\setminus S_{1}|^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[\frac{((k-2)!)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}}{(k-1)!}\left(\xi^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+\left(\frac{1}{(k-2)!}-\xi\right)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\right)n^{k-1}\right].

We obtain (*) by noting that since
x^{(k-1)/(k-2)}
is convex and
|S_{2}|\geq\xi n^{k-2}
, we have

|S_{1}\setminus S_{2}|^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+|S_{2}\setminus S_{1}|^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\leq|S_{1}|^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+\left({n\choose k-2}-|S_{1}|\right)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\leq\left(\xi^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+\left(\frac{1}{(k-2)!}-\xi\right)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\right)n^{k-1}.

•

We choose those non-monotone clauses containing the variable
u
and at least one negated literal that is not
\overline{u}
. By
Lemma

7.4
, there are at most

\left((k-1)!\sum_{t=1}^{\zeta n^{k-1}}{{n\choose k-1}\choose t}\right)^{2(k-1)}=\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k-1}\right]

choices.

•

We choose the clauses not containing
u
, which we can do in at most
|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|
ways.

Consequently the number of
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
with
|T|\leq\theta_{1}n^{k-2}
and
|S_{1}|>\xi n^{k-2}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle n\cdot 4^{{n\choose k-2}}\cdot\exp_{2}\left[2\theta_{1}n^{k-1}+H(\theta_{1})n^{k-1}+\frac{((k-2)!)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}}{(k-1)!}\left(\xi^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}+\left(\frac{1}{(k-2)!}-\xi\right)^{\frac{k-1}{k-2}}\right)n^{k-1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 227.62204pt+O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|,

where (*) is due to the fact that
\theta_{1}
and
\zeta
are sufficiently smaller than
\xi
, and
c
is small.
∎

Combining the above two cases, we have

|\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})|\leq\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|.

∎

7.2.2.
Formulae with lots of clauses
\overline{u_{1}}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}

Next, we bound the number of formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
that have many clauses of the form
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}
for some
2\leq i\leq k-2
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\in X
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Fix some
i\in\{2,\dots,k-2\}
. Then, for any
\beta_{i}>0
, there exist
\beta_{1}>0
,
\zeta>0
and
c>0
such that

|\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,1}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,i}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})|\leq\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|.

Proof.

Fix
i
. For every
\beta_{i}>0
, we choose
\theta_{i}>0
and
\beta_{1}>0
sufficiently smaller than
\beta_{i}
, and then choose
\zeta>0
sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{i}
. Take a small constant
c=c(\beta_{i},\theta_{i},\beta_{1},\zeta)>0
.

For every
G\in\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,i}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})
, by definition, there exist some
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\in X
such that there are more than
\beta_{i}n^{k-i}
clauses of the form
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}
. Let
X_{\mathbf{u}}=X\setminus\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\}
. For every
j\in\{1,\dots,i\}
and
\mathbf{v}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}\subset X_{\mathbf{u}}
, set

N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}=\left\{\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i-1}\}\in{X_{\mathbf{u}}\choose i-1}:u_{j}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}\in G\right\}.

Additionally, let

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}\in{X_{\mathbf{u}}\choose k-i}:\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}\in G\right\},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathbf{v}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}\in M:\left|\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}\right|\geq\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\right\},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=M\setminus A.

Since
|M|>\beta_{i}n^{k-i}
, at least one of the following holds:

(1)

|A|>\theta_{i}n^{k-i}
;

(2)

|B|>(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}
.

Claim 7.10
.

For sufficiently small
c>0
, there are at most
\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]
formulae
G\in\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,i}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})
, with
|A|>\theta_{i}n^{k-i}
for
A
defined as above.

Proof.

Consider one such
G
. Since
G
is minimal, for every
\mathbf{v}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}\in M
and
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i-1}\}\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}
, to avoid the non-minimal subformula

\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}z,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}u_{1},\ldots,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}u_{i},\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}\},

(as it is impossible to satisfy only the first clause), we cannot have
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}z\in G
for any
z\in X
. Hence after specifying (1)
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}
, (2)
A
and (3)
\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}
for every
\mathbf{v}\in A
, we know at least

\frac{1}{i{k\choose i}}\cdot|A|\cdot\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\cdot(n-(k-1))\geq\frac{1}{i{k\choose i}}\cdot\theta_{i}n^{k-i}\cdot\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\cdot(n-(k-1))\geq\frac{\theta_{i}^{2}\cdot k!}{i{k\choose i}}{n\choose k}

monotone clauses that cannot belong to
G
. We can specify some
G
by making the following set of choices.

•

We choose
i
variables
u_{1},\ldots,u_{i}
. This can be done in at most
{n\choose i}
ways.

•

We choose
A
. There are at most
2^{n\choose{k-i}}
possibilities.

•

For each
\mathbf{v}\in A
, we pick
\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}
. There are at most
2^{{n\choose{i-1}}|A|}\leq 2^{n^{k-1}}
ways to make this set of choices.

•

We select the non-monotone clauses in
G
, from at most
\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k}\right]
choices.

•

Finally, we choose the monotone clauses in
G
. We can do this in at most

\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-\frac{\theta_{i}^{2}\cdot k!}{i{k\choose i}}{n\choose k}\right]

ways given the set of monotone clauses excluded by the above argument.

Hence the number of possible
G
is at most

\displaystyle{n\choose i}\cdot 2^{n\choose{k-i}}\cdot 2^{n^{k-1}}\cdot\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k}+{n\choose k}-\frac{\theta_{i}^{2}\cdot k!}{i{k\choose i}}{n\choose k}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right],

where (*) is due to the fact that
\zeta
is sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{i}
, and
c
is small.
∎

Claim 7.11
.

For sufficiently small
c>0
, there are at most
\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|
formulae
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,1}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,i}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})
, with
|B|>(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}
for
B
defined as above.

Proof.

Consider one such
G
.
We set

Q=\left\{\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k-1}\}\in{X_{\mathbf{u}}\choose k-1}:\exists j\in[i]\text{ such that }u_{j}w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}\notin G\right\},

so that for every
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k-1}\}\in Q
, there are at most
2^{i}-1
ways to choose which of
u_{1}w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}
, …,
u_{i}w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}
lie in
G
. Notice that for every
\mathbf{v}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}
, we have

\left\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i},w_{1},\dots,w_{i-1}\}\in{X_{\mathbf{u}}\choose k-1}:\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i-1}\}\notin\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}\right\}\subset Q.

We can fully specify
G
via the following choices.

•

We choose
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}
. There are at most
{n\choose i}
ways to do this.

•

We choose
B
. There are at most
2^{n\choose k-i}
possibilities.

•

We then choose
\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}
for every
\mathbf{v}\in B
. Since every
\mathbf{v}\in B
has
|\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}|<\theta_{i}n^{i-1}
, the number of such choices is bounded above by

\left(\sum_{t=1}^{\theta_{i}n^{i-1}}{{n\choose i-1}\choose t}\right)^{|B|}\leq\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\theta_{i}\log(1/\theta_{i})){n\choose i-1}{n\choose k-i}\right].

•

We choose the monotone clauses involving some
u_{j}
. Since
|B|>(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}
and
|\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}|<\theta_{i}n^{i-1}
for every
\mathbf{v}\in B
, after specifying
B
and
\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}N_{u_{j},\mathbf{v}}
for every
\mathbf{v}\in B
, we know at least

{k-1\choose i-1}^{-1}(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}\left({|X_{\mathbf{u}}\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}|\choose i-1}-\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\right)

(k-1)
-subsets of variables that belong to
Q
. Hence there are at most

\exp_{2}\left[i{n-1\choose k-1}-(i-\log(2^{i}-1)){k-1\choose i-1}^{-1}(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}\left({n-1-(k-i)\choose i-1}-\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\right)\right]

ways of choosing the monotone clauses in
G
that contain some
u_{j}
.

•

We next select the clauses containing some
\overline{u}_{j}
where all other literals are positive. Since
G\in\mathcal{I}_{2,1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})
, there can be at most
\beta_{1}n^{k-1}
such clauses for every
u_{j}
, so the total number of choices is at most

\left(\sum_{t\leq\beta_{1}n^{k-1}}{{n-1\choose k-1}\choose t}\right)^{i}\leq\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\beta_{1}\log(1/\beta_{1})){n-1\choose k-1}\right].

•

We then select the clauses containing some variable
u_{j}
and some negative literal other than
\overline{u}_{j}
. By
Lemma

7.4
, we can choose them in at most
\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k-1}\right]
ways.

•

Finally we select the clauses that do not involve any variable
u_{j}
. There are at most
|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|
choices.

Hence the number of possible
G
is at most

\displaystyle{n\choose i}\cdot 2^{n\choose k-i}\cdot|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\theta_{i}\log(1/\theta_{i})){n\choose i-1}{n\choose k-i}+O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k-1}+O_{k}(\beta_{1}\log(1/\beta_{1})){n-1\choose k-1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad+i{n-1\choose k-1}-(i-\log(2^{i}-1)){k-1\choose i-1}^{-1}(\beta_{i}-\theta_{i})n^{k-i}\left({n-1-(k-i)\choose i-1}-\theta_{i}n^{i-1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|,

where (*) is due to the fact that
\theta_{i}
,
\beta_{1}
and
\zeta
are all sufficiently smaller than
\beta_{i}
, and
c
is small.
∎

Combining the above two cases, we have

|\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,1}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2,i}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1},\beta_{i})|\leq\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|

for sufficiently small
c>0
.
∎

7.3.
Monotone neighborhoods of variables

We build the tools for bounding the number of formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
having a large number of clauses containing either
k-1
or
k
negative literals.
Here we consider the size of the “monotone neighborhoods” of variable
u
and the intersections of these neighborhoods, an idea we make more precise below.

Definition 7.12
.

For every
u\in X
, let
Z_{u}=\{\{v_{1},\ldots v_{k-1}\}\in\binom{X}{k-1}\mid uv_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\in G\}
, i.e., those
(k-1)
-subsets of variables that form a monotone clause with
u
. Let
\overline{Z_{u}}={X\backslash\{u\}\choose k-1}\backslash Z_{u}
, i.e., those
(k-1)
-subsets of variables that do not form a monotone clause with
u
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})

\displaystyle=\left\{G\in\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta}):\forall u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k}\in X,\,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad|Z_{u_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap Z_{u_{k-1}}\cap Z_{u_{k}}|,|Z_{u_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap Z_{u_{k-1}}\cap\overline{Z_{u_{k}}}|,|Z_{u_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap\overline{Z_{u_{k-1}}}\cap\overline{Z_{u_{k}}}|,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\dots,|\overline{Z_{u_{1}}}\cap\cdots\cap\overline{Z_{u_{k-1}}}\cap\overline{Z_{u_{k}}}|\geq\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}\right\}.

Lemma 7.13
.

For all
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k-2})>0
with
\beta_{1}
sufficiently small, there exist
\zeta,c>0
such that

|\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\backslash\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|\leq\exp_{2}\left[k(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|.

Proof.

Fix
\vec{\beta}>0
with
\beta_{1}
sufficiently small. Take
\zeta>0
sufficiently smaller than
\beta_{1}
, and
c=c(\beta_{1},\zeta)>0
. To specify some
G\in\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\backslash\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
, it suffices to pick the following.

•

We choose
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
violating the condition for
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
, which we can do in at most
{n\choose k}
ways.

•

We choose the non-monotone clauses that include at least one of
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
. For every
u_{j}
, since there are at most
\beta_{1}n^{k-1}+2(k-1)\zeta n^{k-1}
such clauses, we choose from at most

\displaystyle\sum_{t\leq(\beta_{1}+2(k-1)\zeta)n^{k-1}}{(2^{k}-1){n-1\choose k-1}\choose t}

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[H\left(\frac{(\beta_{1}+2(k-1)\zeta)n^{k-1}}{(2^{k}-1){n-1\choose k-1}}\right)\cdot(2^{k}-1){n-1\choose k-1}\right]

choices.
This gives in total at most

\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}((\zeta+\beta_{1})\log(1/(\zeta+\beta_{1}))){n\choose k-1}\right]

choices.

•

We pick the monotone clauses involving at least one of
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
. Note that there are
2^{O(n^{k-2})}
choices of subsets of clauses involving at least two of
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
. Meanwhile, the number of monotone clauses involving exactly
1
of
u_{1},\ldots u_{k}
is bounded by the number of choices of an ordered partition of
{X\backslash\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}\choose k-1}
into
2^{k}
parts (i.e., choosing membership in
Z_{u_{i}}
or
\overline{Z_{u_{i}}}
for every
(k-1)
-subset of variables), at least one of which has size less than
\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}
. Note that for
N={n-k\choose k-1}
and
K=2^{k}
, there are
\binom{N}{<\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}N}\leq 2^{H\left(\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)\cdot N}
to choose
Z_{u_{i}}
to be small for any fixed
i
and
(K-1)^{N}
ways to choose membership for the remaining
u_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
The total number of choices is bounded above by

2^{k}\exp_{2}\left[\left(H\left(\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)+\log_{2}(2^{k}-1)\right){n-k\choose k-1}\right].

•

Finally, we choose the clauses not involving any of
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
. We can do this in at most
|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|
ways.

Hence the number of possible
G\in\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\backslash\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
is at most

\displaystyle 2^{k}{n\choose k}\cdot|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|\cdot\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}((\zeta+\beta_{1})\log(1/(\zeta+\beta_{1}))){n\choose k-1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 142.26378pt+\left(H\left(\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)+\frac{2^{k+1}-1}{2^{k+1}}\cdot\log_{2}(2^{k}-1)\right){n-k\choose k-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{<}\exp_{2}\left[k(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|,

where (*) holds since
\zeta
is sufficiently smaller than
\theta_{1}
, and
c
is small.
∎

7.4.
Most formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
are monotone

In this step, we bound the number of formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
that are not monotone. Before stepping into the proof, we first summarize some properties of every
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
that will be useful subsequently.

•

For every
k-1
variables
v_{1},\dots,v_{k-1}
, there are at most
\zeta n
variables
w
such that
v_{1}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
. The same bound holds for
w
’s with
\overline{v}_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
,
w
’s with
v_{1}\overline{v}_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
,
w
’s with
\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}v_{3}\cdots v_{k-1}\overline{w}\in G
, and so on.

•

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, there are at most
\zeta n^{k}
clauses in
G
having exactly
i
negative literals.

•

For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
and
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\}\in{X\choose i}
,
G
contains at most
\beta_{i}n^{k-i}
clauses of the form
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}
;

•

For every
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in X
, we have
|Z_{u_{1}}\cap\dots\cap Z_{u_{k}}|\geq\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}
.

Define
\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
to be the subset of monotone formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
.

Definition 7.14
.

Let

\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})=\{G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta}):\text{$G$ is monotone}\}.

We give an upper bound on
|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|
. The following observation about
\ell
-hypergraphs generalizes a theorem on graphs
[
16
]
(also see
[
27
, Theorem 61.1]
), and will be useful in our proof.

Lemma 7.15
.

Every
\ell
-uniform hypergraph
H
can be directed (i.e., every edge directed at some vertex) such that for every
\ell-1
vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1}
, there are at most

\left\lceil\frac{(\ell-1)!}{(\ell!)^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}}\cdot e(H)^{\frac{1}{\ell}}\right\rceil

edges that contain all of
v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1}
and are directed at a vertex
w
other than
v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1}
.

Proof.

Write

L:=\left\lceil\frac{(\ell-1)!}{(\ell!)^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}}\cdot e(H)^{\frac{1}{\ell}}\right\rceil.

For an
\ell
-graph
H
, construct an associated bipartite graph
B_{H}
in which one part is
E(H)
, the edge set of
H
, and the other part is the multiset
\left\{S^{L}:S\in{V(H)\choose\ell-1}\right\}
. For every
e\in E(H)
and
S\in{V(H)\choose\ell-1}
, we define that
e\sim S
in
B_{H}
if and only if
S\subset e
. Observe that an edge direction of
H
in which every
\ell-1
vertices support at most
L
edges directed at another vertex is equivalent to an
E(H)
-perfect matching in
B_{H}
, which exists if and only if
|W|\leq|N_{B_{H}}(W)|
for every
W\subset E(H)
.

For every
W\subset E(H)
,
N_{B_{H}}(W)
is the set of those
S\in{V(H)\choose\ell-1}
that are contained in some
e\in W
. By the Kruskal–Katona theorem in the form of
Theorem

3.3
, every collection of
(\ell-1)
-vertex sets
\mathcal{S}\subset{V(H)\choose\ell-1}
covers at most
\frac{((\ell-1)!|\mathcal{S}|)^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}}{\ell!}

\ell
-edges, so every
W\subset E(H)
satisfies

\displaystyle|W|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left((\ell-1)!\cdot\frac{|N_{B_{H}}(W)|}{L}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}}}{\ell!},\text{ and }

\displaystyle|N_{B_{H}}(W)|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(\ell!)^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}|W|^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}}{(\ell-1)!}\cdot L\geq\frac{(\ell!)^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}|W|^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}}{(\ell-1)!}\cdot\frac{(\ell-1)!}{(\ell!)^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}}\cdot e(H)^{\frac{1}{\ell}}

\displaystyle=|W|^{\frac{\ell-1}{\ell}}\cdot e(H)^{\frac{1}{\ell}}\geq|W|.\qed

We will also use the following version of Shearer’s inequality
[
8
]
(see also
[
18
, Lemma 6.5]
).

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
W
be a set and
\mathcal{F}
a family of subsets of
W
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a hypergraph with vertex set
W
, with
\deg_{\mathcal{H}}(v)\geq k
for every
v\in W
. Then

\log_{2}|\mathcal{F}|\leq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{A\in\mathcal{H}}\log_{2}|\Tr(\mathcal{F},A)|,

where
\Tr(\mathcal{F},A)=\{F\cap A:F\in\mathcal{F}\}
.

We now show that most
k
-SAT formulae in
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(\vec{\beta},\zeta)
are also in
\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(\vec{\beta},\zeta)
for sufficiently small
\vec{\beta},\zeta
.

Lemma 7.17
.

We can choose
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k-2})
,
\zeta>0
and
c>0
such that

|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(\zeta,\vec{\beta})|<\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-cn^{k-2}\right].

Proof.

Choose sufficiently small
\vec{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k-2})
,
\zeta>0
and
c=c(\vec{\beta},\zeta)>0
.

Observe that every
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
cannot contain any clause with exactly
k-1
negative literals. This is because if
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{k-1}v\in G
, then since there exists some
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k-1}\}\in Z_{u_{1}}\cap\dots\cap Z_{u_{k-1}}\cap Z_{v}
,
G
would have a non-minimal suformula

\{w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}v,w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}u_{1},\dots,w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}u_{k-1},w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}v,\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{k-1}v\}

(as it is impossible to satisfy only the first clause).

Next observe that the number of
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta)
containing a clause with exactly
k
negative literals is at most
\exp_{2}[(1-c){n\choose k}]
. Indeed, if
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{k}\in G
, then for all
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{k-1}\}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}Z_{u_{i}}
, to avoid the non-minimal subformula

\{w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}v,w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}u_{1},\dots,w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}u_{k},\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{k}\}

(as it is impossible to satisfy only the first clause), there cannot be any
v\in X\setminus\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
such that
w_{1}\cdots w_{k-1}v\in G
. Hence after specifying
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
and
\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}Z_{u_{i}}
, we know at least

\frac{1}{k}\cdot(n-2k+1)\left|\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}Z_{u_{i}}\right|\geq\frac{n-2k+1}{k\cdot 2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}

monotone clauses that cannot belong to
G
. We can specify such
G
in the following manner.

•

We choose
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
. There are at most
{n\choose k}
choices.

•

We choose
\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}Z_{u_{i}}
. This can be done in at most
2^{{n\choose k-1}}
ways.

•

We choose the non-monotone clauses in
G
. The number of choices is bounded above by
\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k}\right]
.

•

Finally, we pick the monotone clauses in
G
, of which the number of choices is bounded above by

\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-\frac{n-2k+1}{k\cdot 2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}\right].

Hence the number of possible
G
is at most

{n\choose k}\cdot 2^{{n\choose k-1}}\cdot\exp_{2}\left[O_{k}(\zeta\log(1/\zeta)){n\choose k}+{n\choose k}-\frac{n-2k+1}{k\cdot 2^{k+1}}{n-k\choose k-1}\right]\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right],

where (*) is due to the fact that
\zeta
and
c
are sufficiently small.

Finally, we bound the number of
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
containing no clause with exactly
k-1
or
k
negative literals. For every such
G
, we decompose
G=G_{0}\sqcup G_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup G_{k-2}
, such that every
G_{i}
is the set of clauses in
G
with exactly
i
negative literals. Observe that
|G_{1}|,\dots,|G_{k-2}|\leq\zeta n^{k}
as
G\in\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
. We will prove in a second that:

Claim 7.18
.

For all
t_{1},\dots,t_{k-2}\leq\zeta n^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left\{G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta}):\text{$G$ contains no clause with exactly $k$ negative literals, }\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad|G_{1}|=t_{1},\dots,|G_{k-2}|=t_{k-2}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle<\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-2cn^{n-2}\right].

Since the number of choices of
t_{1},\dots,t_{k-2}
is at most
(\zeta n^{k})^{k-2}
, given
7.18
, we know that there are at most
(\zeta n^{k})^{k-2}\cdot\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-2cn^{k-2}\right]
formulae in
|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|
containing no clause with exactly
k-1
or
k
negative literals. Combining this with the previous two observations, we have the desired bound

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle\leq(\zeta n^{k})^{k-2}\cdot\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-2cn^{k-2}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-cn^{k-2}\right].

∎

Proof of
7.18
.

Consider some
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
such that
G
contains no clause with exactly
k-1
or
k
negative literals, and
|G_{1}|=t_{1}
,…,
|G_{k-2}|=t_{k-2}
.

For every
i\in[k-2]
, we construct an auxiliary collection
G_{i}^{\prime}\subset{X\choose i}\times{X\choose k-i-1}\times X
based on
G_{i}
, by doing the following: for every clause
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}\in G_{i}
, we pick one
j\in\{1,\dots,k-i\}
and put the element
(\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\},\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j-1},v_{j+1},\dots,v_{k-i}\},v_{j})
in
G_{i}^{\prime}
. In other words, we obtain
G_{i}^{\prime}
from
G_{i}
by making the set of positive literals in every clause “directed" at some
v_{j}
.

There are clearly many ways to build
G_{i}^{\prime}
. We claim that there exists some
G_{i}^{\prime}
such that for every
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\in X
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\in X\setminus\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\}
, there are at most

\left\lceil\frac{(k-i-1)!}{((k-i)!)^{\frac{k-i-1}{k-i}}}\cdot\beta_{i}^{\frac{1}{k-i}}\cdot n\right\rceil

elements of the form
(\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\},\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},w)
. For every
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\in X
, define the
(k-i)
-hypergraph
H
by

\displaystyle V(H)

\displaystyle=X\setminus\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\},

\displaystyle E(H)

\displaystyle=\{\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i}\}:\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i}\in G_{i}\}.

Since
G
contains at most
\beta_{i}n^{k-i}
clauses of the form
\overline{u}_{1}\dots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\dots v_{k-i}
, we have
e(H)\leq\beta_{i}n^{k-i}
, so by
Lemma

7.15
we know that
H
can be directed such that every
v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\in X\setminus\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\}
are contained in at most
\left\lceil\frac{(k-i-1)!}{((k-i)!)^{\frac{k-i-1}{k-i}}}\cdot\beta_{i}^{\frac{1}{k-i}}\cdot n\right\rceil
edges directed at some other vertex. This gives us the
G_{i}^{\prime}
as wanted.

Now we fix this
G_{i}^{\prime}
. Define the
(i+1)
-multigraph
H_{i}
by

\displaystyle V(H_{i})

\displaystyle={X\choose k-i},

\displaystyle E(H_{i})

\displaystyle=\{\{\{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\dots,\{u_{i},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\{w,v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\}\}:

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt(\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i}\},\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},w)\in G_{i}^{\prime}\}.

For every
e=\{\{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\dots,\{u_{i},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\{w,v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\}\}\in E(H_{i})
and any other
i
variables
a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\notin\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1},w\}
, since
\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}w\in G_{i}
, to avoid the non-minimal subformula

\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}w,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{1},\dots,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{i},\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{i}v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}w\}

(as it is impossible to satisfy only the first clause), we have

G_{0}\cap\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}w,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{1},\dots,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{i}\}<i+1.

Define the formula
F(e,a_{1},\dots,a_{i})
by

F(e,a_{1},\dots,a_{i})=\{v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}w,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{1},\dots,v_{1}\cdots v_{k-i-1}a_{1}\cdots a_{i}u_{i}\}.

We also observe that given any vertex
\mathbf{v}\in V(H_{i})
, we have

\deg_{H_{i}}(\mathbf{v})\leq(k-i)\left({n\choose i-1}\cdot\left\lceil\frac{(k-i-1)!}{((k-i)!)^{\frac{k-i-1}{k-i}}}\cdot\beta_{i}^{\frac{1}{k-i}}\cdot n\right\rceil+{n\choose i-1}\cdot\zeta n\right).

This is because when
\mathbf{v}
takes the role of (say)
\{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\}
in an edge, to identify an edge containing
\mathbf{v}
, it suffices to:

•

identify
u_{1}
from the
k-i
variables in
\mathbf{v}
;

•

choose
u_{2},\dots,u_{i}\in X
from at most
{n\choose i-1}
choices;

•

choose
w
from at most
\left\lceil\frac{(k-i-1)!}{((k-i)!)^{\frac{k-i-1}{k-i}}}\cdot\beta_{i}^{\frac{1}{k-i}}\cdot n\right\rceil
choices, according to the property of
G_{i}^{\prime}
.

When
\mathbf{v}
takes the role of
\{w,v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\}
in an edge, to identify an edge containing
\mathbf{v}
, it suffices to:

•

identify
w
from the
k-i
variables in
\mathbf{v}
;

•

choose the lexicographically smallest
i-1
variables among
u_{1},\dots,u_{i}
from at most
{n\choose i-1}
choices;

•

choose the remaining variable from at most
\zeta n
choices, according to
Lemma

7.4
.

Let
\nu_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
denote the matching and vertex cover numbers of
H_{i}
. Choose
\rho_{i}>0
sufficiently small, yet still larger than
\beta_{i}
and
\zeta
such that we have the degree bound

\deg_{H_{i}}(\mathbf{v})\leq(k-i)\left({n\choose i-1}\cdot\left\lceil\frac{(k-i-1)!}{((k-i)!)^{\frac{k-i-1}{k-i}}}\cdot\beta_{i}^{\frac{1}{k-i}}\cdot n\right\rceil+{n\choose i-1}\cdot\zeta n\right)\leq\rho_{i}n^{i}

for every
\mathbf{v}\in V(H_{i})
. We then have the inequalities

(i+1)\nu_{i}\geq\tau_{i}\geq\frac{t_{i}}{\rho_{i}n^{i}}.

Given
t_{i}
,
\nu_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
, the number of possible
G_{i}
is bounded by the product of:

•

{{n\choose k-i}\choose\tau_{i}}\leq{n^{k-i}\choose\tau_{i}}
(choosing a vertex cover
\mathcal{T}_{i}
of
H_{i}
);

•

{{k\choose i}\tau_{i}{n\choose i}\choose t_{i}}
(choosing a collection of
t_{i}
clauses, each using at least one member of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
).

Suppose
G_{1},\dots,G_{k-2}
have been determined and consider the number of possible
G_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{i}
be some maximum matching of
H_{i}
; say
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\{e_{j}:j\in[\nu_{i}]\}
.
Define the family of monotone formulae
\mathcal{J}_{i}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j=1}^{\nu_{i}}\{F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i}):a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\notin\{u_{1},\dots,u_{i},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1},w\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ with }e_{j}=\{\{u_{1},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\dots,\{u_{i},v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\},\{w,v_{1},\dots,v_{k-i-1}\}\}\}.

For every possible
G_{0}
and every
F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i})\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, we have
|G_{0}\cap F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i})|<i+1.

Moreover, since every
F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i})
is of the form
\{y_{1}\cdots y_{k-1}z_{1},\dots,y_{1}\cdots y_{k-1}z_{i+1}\}
, and every formula of the form
\{y_{1}\cdots y_{k-1}z_{1},\dots,y_{1}\cdots y_{k-1}z_{i+1}\}
can come from at most
{k-1\choose i}
different
e_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
, we have

|\mathcal{J}_{i}|\geq\frac{\nu_{i}{n-k\choose i}}{{k-1\choose i}}.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a (non-uniform) multigraph, whose vertex set is the set of all monotone clauses on variable set
X
, and whose edge set is the union of all
\mathcal{J}_{i}
and a number of single-clause formulae, such that every monotone clause lies in exactly
\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}{k-1\choose i}
edges. In other words, we have

\displaystyle V(\mathcal{H})={X\choose k},\qquad E(\mathcal{H})=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k-2}\mathcal{J}_{i}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{C\in{X\choose k}}\{C\}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\left({k-1\choose i}-\eta_{i}(C)\right)}\right),

where
\eta_{i}(C)\leq{k-1\choose i}
is the number of edges in
\mathcal{J}_{i}
that contain
C
. Notice that every vertex in
\mathcal{H}
has degree exactly
\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}{k-1\choose i}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{0}
be the collection of all possible
G_{0}
. By
Lemma

7.16
, we have

\log_{2}|\mathcal{G}_{0}|\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}{k-1\choose i}\right)^{-1}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{J}_{i}}\log_{2}|\Tr(\mathcal{G}_{0},F)|=\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{J}_{i}}\log_{2}|\Tr(\mathcal{G}_{0},F)|.

For all
F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i})\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, since
|G_{0}\cap F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i})|<i+1
for all
G_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
, we have
|\text{Tr}(\mathcal{G}_{0},F(e_{j},a_{1},\dots,a_{i}))|\leq 2^{i+1}-1
. Since we also have all
\text{Tr}\left(\mathcal{G}_{0},\{T\}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\left({k-1\choose i}-\eta_{i}(T)\right)}\right)\leq 2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\log_{2}|\mathcal{G}_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{C\in{V\choose k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\left({k-1\choose i}-\eta_{i}(C)\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}|\mathcal{J}_{i}|\log_{2}(2^{i+1}-1)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}{n\choose k}-\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}(i+1-\log_{2}(2^{i+1}-1))|\mathcal{J}_{i}|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(**)}{\leq}{n\choose k}-\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\frac{(i+1-\log_{2}(2^{i+1}-1)){n-k\choose i}\nu_{i}}{{k-1\choose i}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(***)}{\leq}{n\choose k}-\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\frac{(i+1-\log_{2}(2^{i+1}-1)){n-k\choose i}\tau_{i}}{(i+1){k-1\choose i}}\right)

where (*) is because
\sum_{T\in{V\choose k}}\eta_{i}(T)=(i+1)|\mathcal{J}_{i}|
, (**) is because
|\mathcal{J}_{i}|\geq\frac{\nu_{i}{n-k\choose i}}{{k-1\choose i}}
, and (***) is because
\frac{\tau_{i}}{i+1}\leq\nu_{i}
. For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, set

\delta_{i}=\left(2^{k-1}-2\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{i+1-\log_{2}(2^{i+1}-1)}{(i+1){k-1\choose i}}>0.

Given all the
t_{i}
,
\nu_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
, combining the above with the previous observation that there are at most
{n^{k-i}\choose\tau_{i}}{{k\choose i}\tau_{i}{n\choose i}\choose t_{i}}
possible
G_{i}
, we obtain that the number of possible
G\in\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
is at most

\displaystyle\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\delta_{i}{n-k\choose i}\tau_{i}\right)\right]\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}{n^{k-i}\choose\tau_{i}}{{k\choose i}\tau_{i}{n\choose i}\choose t_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\left(O_{k}(\log n)\tau_{i}+H\left(\frac{t_{i}}{{k\choose i}\tau_{i}{n\choose i}}\right)\tau_{i}n^{i}-\delta_{i}{n-k\choose i}\tau_{i}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\leq}\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\left(O_{k}(\log n)+H\left(\frac{\rho_{i}\cdot 2(i!)}{{k\choose i}}\right)n^{i}-\delta_{i}{n-k\choose i}\right)\tau_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-3cn^{k-2}\right],

where (*) is due to the fact that
\tau_{i}\geq\frac{t_{i}}{\rho_{i}n^{i}}
. For every
i\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
, there are at most
\frac{{n\choose k-i}}{i+1}
possible values of
\nu_{i}
and at most
{n\choose k-i}
possible values of
\tau_{i}
. Hence the number of possible
G\in\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})
given
t_{1},\dots,t_{k-2}
is at most
\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-3cn^{k-2}\right]\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}\frac{{n\choose k-i}}{i+1}{n\choose k-i}\right)\leq\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-2cn^{k-2}\right]
.
∎

7.5.
Bound on
|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Decomposing
\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})|+|\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|+|\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle+|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|+|\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|.

Recall that we have the following inequalities from a combination of
Lemma

7.3
,
Lemma

7.6
,
Lemma

7.9
,
Lemma

7.13
and
Lemma

7.17
, where
\vec{\beta}>0
chosen so that
\beta_{1}
is sufficiently small (in general and also with respect to
\beta_{2},\ldots\beta_{k}
),
\zeta>0
is sufficiently small relative to
\vec{\beta}
, and
c^{\prime}>c>0
are constants chosen to be small relative to
\zeta
and
\vec{\beta}
.

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}{n-1\choose k-1}\right]\left\lvert\mathcal{I}(n-1)\right\rvert,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1}^{*}(n,\zeta,\beta_{1})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle\leq(k-2)\exp_{2}\left[(1-c^{\prime}){n\choose k}\right]+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c^{\prime}){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-1)|

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=2}^{k-2}\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c^{\prime}){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{2}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[k(1-c^{\prime}){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{3}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})\setminus\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-c^{\prime}n^{k-2}\right],

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{4}^{*}(n,\zeta,\vec{\beta})|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{{n\choose k}}.

Combining the above inequalities gives the desired upper bound.

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n\choose k}+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c){n\choose k}\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\exp_{2}\left[i(1-c){n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-i)|

\displaystyle\qquad+\exp_{2}\left[(1-c)k{n\choose k-1}\right]|\mathcal{I}(n-k)|+\exp_{2}\left[{n\choose k}-cn^{k-2}\right].\qed

This immediately gives the desired tight asymptotic.

Proof of Theorem
1.24
.

Choose some
\theta>\log_{2}3
with
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
and some
\zeta<\zeta_{0}
as in
Theorem

7.1
. We then choose
\epsilon>0
as in
6.9
.

Recall from
6.9
that for
n
sufficiently large, we have

|\mathcal{I}(n)|\leq 2^{\left(1-\epsilon\right)\binom{n}{k}}+2^{n}\left\lvert\mathcal{I}^{*}(n,\zeta)\right\rvert.

Then, substituting the bound in
Theorem

7.1
, we obtain by induction that for some constant
\Delta,c_{0}>0
and all
n
,

(7.1)

\left\lvert\mathcal{I}(n)\right\rvert\leq(1+\Delta\cdot 2^{-c_{0}n})B(n),

where
B(n)=2^{{n\choose k}+n}
.
∎

Appendix A
Brute force verification of Conjecture
1.1
for
k=5
(by Nitya Mani and Edward Yu)

A.1.
Overview

As observed in Remark
1.28
, to verify that
\pi(\mathcal{F}_{k},\theta)=1
for some fixed
k
and some
\theta>\log_{2}3
,
it suffices to show that for some constant
n>k
,
\alpha(\vec{H})+(\log_{2}3)\beta(\vec{H})\leq 1
over all
\vec{T}_{k}
-free
k
-PDGs
\vec{H}
on exactly
n
vertices (with
\alpha(\vec{H})\binom{n}{k}
undirected edges and
\beta(\vec{H})\binom{n}{k}
directed edges).

This is a finite check for any fixed
k
. The new result for 5-SAT relies on
(n,k)=(7,4)
, which took 6600 CPU hours (in parallel) after several optimizations. We describe the algorithm used to efficiently enumerate
7
-vertex
4
-PDGs and verify they were
\vec{T}_{4}
-free. The complete, documented code (along with installation and running instructions) can be found at
https://github.com/ThinGarfield/Density-k-PDG/tree/v10.1
.

Using this code, we computed for the following pairs
(n,k)
, the largest value of
\theta
(which appears in the corresponding table entry) such that
\alpha(\vec{H})+\theta\beta(\vec{H})\leq 1
for all
n
-vertex
\vec{T}_{k}
-free
k
-PDGs
\vec{H}
.

to observe that

.

A.2.
n=7,k=4

Our computation enables us to verify Conjecture
1.1
for
k=5
.

Proposition A.1
.

We have that
\pi(\vec{T}_{5},\theta)=1
for some
\theta>\log_{2}3
. Consequently the number of
5
-SAT functions is
(1+o(1))2^{n+{n\choose k}}
.
