455.45732pt}\})

By subsampling, this verifies that
\pi(\vec{T}_{4},7/4)=1
. Applying Lemma
2.1
, we find that

1\geq\pi(\vec{T}_{4},7/4)\geq\pi\left(\vec{T}_{5},\frac{4\cdot 7/4+1}{5}\right)=\pi(\vec{T}_{5},8/5).

Taking
\theta=8/5>\log_{2}3
, the desired result follows from Theorem
1.24
.
∎

A.3.
Compute environment

We implement this search in C++ 17 (specific details about the development environment and installation can be found in the code README).

We use the Google Cloud Platform Compute Engine to enumerate
k
-PDGs. Below we summarize the runtime for fixed
n,k
of computing the maximal
\theta
such that
\alpha(\vec{H})+\theta\beta(\vec{H})\leq 1
for all
n
-vertex,
\vec{T}_{k}
-free
k
-PDGs.

•

n=7,k=3
took 880 CPU hours

•

n=7,k=4
(the primary desired case) took 6600 CPU hours in 30 batches, checking 29313 base graphs (described in more detail in the following subsection)

•

n=7,k=5
took a few minutes on a regular computer

•

n=8,k=5
is not computationally tractable with the current algorithm (would require
\sim 10^{15}
CPU hours with the current implementation and checking 4722759 base graphs). We think
n=9,k=5
, an even more expensive computation, is the minimum
n
that might verify Conjecture
1.1
for
k=6
.

•

n=8,k=6
took 200 CPU hours

A.4.
Algorithm

We outline the algorithm below. Further documentation and associated testing can be found in the codebase.

Algorithm A.2
.

Principally, we enumerate in a way to reduce the number of “base
k
-PDGs” that must be checked by growing a search tree of
k
-PDGs and eliminate unnecessary steps in computation. These optimizations are crucial for making the problem more computationally tractable.

(1)

Initialization: Let
\mathcal{G}_{k-1}
be the set comprising the single PDG formed by taking a collection of vertices
\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
with no edges.

(2)

For
\ell\in\{k,k+1,\ldots,n-1\}
:

•

Initialize
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:=\left\{(V(\vec{G})\cup\{\ell\},E(\vec{G})):\vec{G}\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell-1}\right\}

•

For each
\vec{G}\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
:

–

Consider all possible combinations of edges (undirected or directed towards any vertex) that could be added to each
\vec{G}\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
that include vertex
\ell
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
be the collection of possible edge sets to add.

–

For each potential collection of edges
F\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
, consider candidate
\vec{H}_{F}=(V(\vec{G}),E(\vec{G})\cup F)
, and check if
\vec{H}
is
\vec{T}_{k}
-free. If not, discard the associated
\vec{H}_{F}
. If so, express
\vec{H}_{F}
in a
canonical form
, which is a representation chosen to ease in tracking non-isomorphic
k
-PDGs.

–

For each
\vec{T}_{k}
-free
\vec{H}_{F}
not isomorphic to some existing element in
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
, add
\vec{H}_{F}
to
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
.

•

Sort
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
with respect to the canonical form.

(3)

Repeat the above process for
\ell=n
, but this time do not represent PDGs in canonical form or check for isomorphisms (but verify that the
k
-PDGs in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
are
\vec{T}_{k}
-free)

(4)

Return the maximum value of
\theta
such that for all
\vec{H}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
,
\alpha(\vec{H})+\theta\beta(\vec{H})\leq 1
.

There are several implemented optimizations (verified by a variety of tests, outlined in the code
README
) that make the above algorithm computationally tractable. We outline some of the most important ones below.

(1)

We represent
k
-PDGs with a data structure that contains edges, vertex signatures, and a graph hash without pointer indirections, representing edges with a 16-bit struct. This representation makes it faster to verify that a
k
-PDG is
\vec{T}_{k}
-free, check for isomorphisms, and copy and grow
k
-PDGs.

(2)

As we grow our classes
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
, for every
\vec{G}\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
we keep track of the largest
\theta
such that
\alpha(\vec{G})+\theta\beta(\vec{G})\leq 1
.

(3)

We use bit mask manipulation and a bit representation of edges to efficiently verify if a
k
-PDG is
\vec{T}_{k}
-free. We also attempt to limit the number of supersets of edge sets that contain a copy of
\vec{T}_{k}
that are checked.

(4)

We adapt some graph isomorphism checking strategies
[
24
,
25
]
to
k
-PDGs by hashing degree information of vertices in a current
k
-PDG in a permutation invariant way (which is maintained via a canonical form to represent
k
-PDGs). We call this hash the
vertex signature
. We also compute a whole
k
-PDG
graph hash.
These steps make it easier to verify if two
k
-PDGs are isomorphic (although both of these sets of hashes could match for non-isomorphic
k
-PDGs, in which case we check for the
k
-PDGs being identical as we walk through permutations that align in vertex signature).
